Reconstructing a graph from its Bell colouring graph
Brian Hearn

Abstract
The Bell colouring graph ℬ ⁡ ( G ) \mathcal{B}(G) of a graph G G is the graph whose vertices are the partitions of the vertex set of G G into independent sets, with an edge between two partitions if and only if one can be obtained from the other by changing the part of a single vertex of G G . Given a natural number k k , the Bell k k -colouring graph ℬ k ​ ( G ) \mathcal{B}_{k}(G) and the upper-Bell k k -colouring graph ℬ ≥ k ​ ( G ) \mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G) are the induced subgraphs of ℬ ⁡ ( G ) \mathcal{B}(G) consisting of all partitions with at most k k parts and at least k k parts, respectively. We determine precisely when two finite graphs have isomorphic Bell colouring graphs. In particular, we show that every n n -vertex graph G G with no vertices of degree n − 1 n-1 is uniquely determined by its Bell colouring graph ℬ ⁡ ( G ) \mathcal{B}(G) , and by its upper-Bell colouring graph ℬ ≥ k ​ ( G ) \mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G) if k ≤ n − 2 k\leq n-2 . We also show that every n n -vertex graph with maximum degree Δ ⁡ ( G ) < 1 9 ​ n − 1 3 \Delta(G)<\frac{1}{9}n-\frac{1}{3} is uniquely determined by its Bell k k -colouring graph ℬ k ​ ( G ) \mathcal{B}_{k}(G) if k > χ ⁡ ( G ) k>\chi(G) . By taking graph complements, each of these results can be restated in terms of partitions into cliques.



1
Introduction and main results

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a finite graph and
k
a natural number.
An
independent set partition
of
G
is a partition of
V(G)
into independent sets.
The
Bell colouring graph
\mathcal{B}(G)
is the graph whose vertex set is the set of independent set partitions of
G
, with an edge between two partitions if and only if one can be obtained from the other by changing the part of a single vertex of
G
(either moving a vertex into a different existing part or creating a new part of size
1
).
Observe that
\mathcal{B}(G)
is a finite graph.
The
Bell
k
-colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
is the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{B}(G)
whose vertex set is the set of independent set partitions of
G
with at most
k
parts.
Observe that
\mathcal{B}(G)=\mathcal{B}_{\lvert V(G)\rvert}(G)
.
We will also consider the
upper-Bell
k
-colouring graph

\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
, the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{B}(G)
whose vertex set is the set of independent set partitions of
G
with at least
k
parts.
The term Bell
k
-colouring graph, introduced in Finbow and MacGillivray
[
8
]
, comes from the
k
-Bell number
of a graph
G
, defined as the number of partitions of
V(G)
into at most
k
independent sets.
This is, in turn, a generalisation of the classical Bell number
B_{n}
of an integer
n
, the number of partitions of the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

A (proper)
k
-colouring
of
G
is a map from
V(G)
to
\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
such that no adjacent vertices are assigned the same number, i.e. ‘colour’.
The
chromatic number

\chi(G)
of
G
is the least number of colours required for a proper colouring of
G
.
The
k
-recolouring graph
\mathcal{C}_{k}(G)
is the graph whose vertex set is the set of
k
-colourings of
G
, and whose edges link pairs of
k
-colourings which differ at exactly one vertex of
G
.

Every
k
-colouring of
G
partitions the vertices of
G
into at most
k
independent sets.
Therefore, independent set partitions can be thought of as ‘unlabelled’ colourings, making Bell
k
-colouring graphs close relatives of
k
-recolouring graphs.
The latter have been well-studied; for example, results on connectivity and Hamiltonicity can be found in
[
7
,
5
,
6
]
.
Subsequently, Bell
k
-colouring graphs have been studied in their own right, for example in Haas
[
9
]
and Finbow and MacGillivray
[
8
]
, the latter showing that
\mathcal{B}(G)
is Hamiltonian unless
G
is a complete graph or a complete graph minus an edge.
Surveys on these
reconfiguration
problems in general include
[
13
,
12
]
.

In this paper, we are interested in determining when
G
is uniquely determined by its Bell colouring, Bell
k
-colouring, or upper-Bell
k
-colouring graphs.
We are motivated by recent similar results for
k
-recolouring graphs
[
11
,
4
,
2
]
.
Our main results are as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
G
be a graph with no vertices of degree
\lvert V(G)\rvert-1
.
Then
G
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{B}(G)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
G
be a graph with maximum degree
\Delta(G)<\frac{1}{9}\lvert V(G)\rvert-\frac{1}{3}
.
Then
G
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
if
k>\chi(G)
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
G
be a graph with no vertices of degree
\lvert V(G)\rvert-1
.
Then
G
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
if
k\leq\lvert V(G)\rvert-2
.

We will prove each of the above theorems by giving explicit algorithms for reconstructing
G
.
In each case, it is not necessary to know the precise value of
k
so long as the relevant conditions are satisfied.

Call a vertex of
G

universal
if it has degree
\lvert V(G)\rvert-1
.
To see why it is impossible to detect universal vertices of
G
from its Bell colouring graph
\mathcal{B}(G)
, observe that such a vertex must always belong to a part of size
1
in any independent set partition of
G
.
Therefore, adding a universal vertex to
G
does not change its Bell colouring graph up to isomorphism.
With this in mind, Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
are respectively special cases of the following two results.

Theorem 1.4
.

Two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
have isomorphic Bell colouring graphs if and only if the graphs obtained from
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
by removing all universal vertices are isomorphic.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be graphs and let
k_{i}\leq\lvert V(G_{i})\rvert-2
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
if and only if the graphs obtained from
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
by removing all universal vertices are isomorphic, and either
k_{i}\leq\chi(G_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, or
\lvert V(G_{1})\rvert-k_{1}=\lvert V(G_{2})\rvert-k_{2}
.

In particular, if we are told
\lvert V(G)\rvert
, then any graph
G
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{B}(G)
, or by
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
so long as we know that
k\leq\lvert V(G)\rvert-2
.
Moreover, with some additional work, Theorem
1.5
can be strengthened to give a complete classification of the pairs of tuples
(G_{1},k_{1})
and
(G_{2},k_{2})
such that
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
.
This is Theorem
A.1
in Appendix
A
.

Recently, the question of whether a given (unlabelled)
k
-recolouring graph
\mathcal{C}_{k}(G)
uniquely determines
G
has been asked and studied in
[
1
,
11
,
4
,
2
]
.
Subsequently, Asgarli et al.
[
3
]
showed that every tree
T
is uniquely determined by its (unlabelled) Bell
3
-colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{3}(T)
, and that every graph
G
is uniquely determined, up to any universal vertices, from its so-called (unlabelled) Bell colouring multigraph
\mathcal{B}^{\kern 0.40974pt\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{

, the multigraph obtained from
\mathcal{B}(G)
by adding multiple edges if there are multiple ways to move between two of its vertices
[
3
]
.
In light of the latter result, Theorem
1.1
shows that the multiple edges of
\mathcal{B}^{\kern 0.40974pt\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{
\hbox to6.07pt{\vbox to3.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.9pt\lower-1.64127pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 1.15 1.99 4.75 1.99 5.91 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\!(G)
are not necessary to determine
G
up to any universal vertices.
Let
P^{*}
denote the partition of
V(G)
into
\lvert V(G)\rvert
parts, each of size
1
.
The strategy employed in Asgarli et al. to reconstruct
G
from
\mathcal{B}^{\kern 0.40974pt\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{

is to use the multiple edges in

to recognise

, from which
G
can be recovered by studying the structure of
\mathcal{B}^{\kern 0.40974pt\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{

locally near
P^{*}
.
However, without these multiple edges available to us, we do not see any way of determining
P^{*}
among the vertices of
\mathcal{B}(G)
, and therefore require significantly more involved arguments to prove Theorem
1.1
.
We discuss this in more detail in Section
2
.

Turning our attention to Theorem
1.2
, recently Asgarli et al.
[
2
]
and Berthe et al.
[
4
]
independently showed that any graph is uniquely determined by its
k
-recolouring graph
\mathcal{C}_{k}(G)
so long as
k>\chi(G)
, even without knowing the precise value of
k
, and showed that this is not true in general if
k\leq\chi(G)
.
The same cannot be true in general for Bell
k
-colouring graphs since, if
G^{+}
is the graph obtained from
G
by adding a universal vertex, then
\chi(G^{+})=\chi(G)+1
and
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}(G^{+})\cong\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
for every natural number
k
.
However, we conjecture the following strengthening of Theorem
1.2
.

Conjecture 1.6
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be graphs and let
k_{i}>\chi(G_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then
\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}(G_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{k_{2}}(G_{2})
are isomorphic if and only if the graphs obtained from
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
by removing all universal vertices are isomorphic, and either
k_{i}\geq\lvert V(G_{i})\rvert
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, or
\lvert V(G_{1})\rvert-k_{1}=\lvert V(G_{2})\rvert-k_{2}
.

The requirement in Theorem
1.2
that
k>\chi(G)
is the best possible in the sense that a graph
G
is not necessarily uniquely determined from
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
if
k\leq\chi(G)
.
Indeed, if
k<\chi(G)
, then
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
has no vertices.
If
k=\chi(G)
, Berthe et al.
[
4
]
gives several examples of families of graphs which are not uniquely determined by
\mathcal{C}_{\chi(G)}(G)
, and it is straightforward to see that these graphs are not uniquely determined by
\mathcal{B}_{\chi(G)}(G)
either.
We restate only the simplest examples here in the context of independent set partitions: any connected bipartite graph
G
on at least two vertices has a unique partition into
\chi(G)=2
independent sets, and any
k
-tree

T
(i.e. any graph formed by starting with a
(k+1)
-clique and repeatedly adding vertices adjacent to some existing
k
-clique) has a unique partition into
\chi(T)=k+1
independent sets.
Observe that in both examples,
\Delta(G)
can be made arbitrarily small relative to
n
.
Two related natural questions for further research are as follows.
First, given a Bell
k
-colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, can we determine if
k=\chi(G)
?
We note that the analogous question for
k
-recolouring graphs was recently answered in
[
2
]
.
Second, which graphs
G
are uniquely determined by their Bell
\chi(G)
-colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{\chi(G)}(G)
?

The condition that
\Delta(G)<\frac{1}{9}\lvert V(G)\rvert-\frac{1}{3}
in Theorem
1.2
is used to guarantee the existence of at least one partition of
V(G)
into
\chi(G)
independent sets, where each independent set has size at least
4
.
The existence of such a partition will allow us to determine
G
from
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
so long as
k>\chi(G)
.
One may therefore replace the maximum degree condition in Theorem
1.2
with any condition which guarantees the existence of such a partition.
Additionally, we do not claim that this maximum degree condition is the best possible to guarantee such a partition.
Note however that such a partition certainly does not exist in general if
\Delta\geq\frac{1}{4}(\lvert V(G)\rvert-1)
, as can be seen by letting
G
be the disjoint union of four cliques, three of size
\frac{1}{4}(\lvert V(G)\rvert-1)
and one of size
\frac{1}{4}(\lvert V(G)\rvert+3)
.
Therefore, a fundamentally different approach would be required in order to prove Conjecture
1.6
in its entirety.
Indeed, using a more involved argument than the one presented in this paper, we can improve the maximum degree condition in the statement of Theorem
1.2
to
\Delta(G)<\frac{1}{2}\lvert V(G)\rvert
in the case where
k>\chi(G)+1
.
However, we do not include this argument here, as it does not bring us significantly closer to proving Conjecture
1.6
in its entirety.

As usual, we will denote the complement of a graph
G
by
\overline{G}
.
We conclude this introduction by noting that every independent set partition of
G
is also a partition of the vertices of the complement
\overline{G}
into cliques.
Therefore, each theorem in this paper can be easily restated in the language of such partitions.

2
Proof of Theorems
1.1
,
1.3
,
1.4
, and
1.5

In this section we will prove Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
, from which Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
immediately follow.
Let
G
be a graph on
n
vertices and let
k
be a natural number.

We begin by defining the following terminology which will be used throughout this paper.
Let
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
or
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
.
We will not distinguish between vertices of
\mathcal{B}
and the partitions of
V(G)
which they represent.

Let
P,Q\in V(\mathcal{B})
be distinct, let
A,B\in P
be distinct, and let
u\in A
be given.
We say
Q
is obtained from
P
by
adding

u
to
B
if
Q
is obtained from
P
by replacing the pair
A,B\in P
with either the pair
A\backslash\{u\},B\cup\{u\}
(in the case where
A\neq\{u\}
) or with just
B\cup\{u\}
(in the case where
A=\{u\}
).
We say that
Q
is a
split neighbour
of
P
, or the
u
-split neighbour
of
P
, or that
Q
is obtained from
P
by
splitting

u
, if
Q
is obtained from
P
by replacing
A
with
A\backslash\{u\}
and
\{u\}
(observe that this requires that
\lvert A\rvert\geq 2
).
We will say that
Q
is obtained from
P
by
moving

u
if
Q
is obtained from
P
either by splitting
u
or by adding
u
to some part of
P
distinct from
A
.
Observe that this relation is symmetric, so we may equivalently say that
P
is obtained from
Q
by moving
u
.
Finally, if
Q
is obtained from
P
by replacing two parts
\{u\},\{v\}\in P
with the part
\{u,v\}
, we say
Q
is obtained from
P
by
merging

\{u\}
and
\{v\}
.

Given a vertex
v\in V(G)
, we will denote the unique part of
P
containing
v
by
P(v)
.
We will use
N(P)
(respectively,
N[P]
) to denote the
open neighbourhood
(respectively,
closed neighbourhood
) of
P
, the subset of
V(\mathcal{B})
consisting of all neighbours of
P
(respectively, consisting of
P
and all neighbours of
P
).
We will use
\mathcal{N}(P)
as shorthand for
\mathcal{B}[N(P)]
, the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{B}
on vertex set
N(P)
.
We will likewise use
\mathcal{N}[P]
as shorthand for
\mathcal{B}[N[P]]
.

2.1
Proof Overview

For any graph
G
, define
G^{\prime}
to be the graph obtained from
G
by removing all of its universal vertices.
Theorem
1.4
then claims that for any graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, we have
\mathcal{B}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}(G_{2})
if and only if
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
.
The ‘if’ direction of Theorem
1.4
follows immediately from our observation in the introduction that adding a universal vertex to a graph
G
does not change its Bell colouring graph
\mathcal{B}(G)
up to isomorphism.
Therefore, to complete the proof of Theorem
1.4
, it remains to show that, given a Bell colouring graph
\mathcal{B}(G)
, one can determine
G^{\prime}
.
We will show in fact the stronger statement that one can determine
G^{\prime}
from the upper-Bell colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
if
k\leq n-2
.
A brief argument will then complete the proof of Theorem
1.5
; this can be found in Subsection
2.4
.

For the rest of Section
2
, fix a graph
G
on
n
vertices and a natural number
k
, and let either
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}(G)
, or
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
with
k\leq n-1
, unless stated otherwise.

Let
P^{*}
denote the partition of the vertices of
G
into
n
parts of size
1
.
The strategy used in Asgarli et al.
[
3
]
for determining
G^{\prime}
from its Bell colouring multigraph
\mathcal{B}^{\kern 0.40974pt\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{

Every neighbour of

{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 M 7.15 0 C 7.15 0.69 6.59 1.25 5.91 1.25 C 5.22 1.25 4.66 0.69 4.66 0 C 4.66 -0.69 5.22 -1.25 5.91 -1.25 C 6.59 -1.25 7.15 -0.69 7.15 0 Z M 5.91 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\!(G)
, there are two edges between
P^{*}
and each of its neighbours.
This special property allows for
P^{*}
to be recognised without too much difficulty.
Indeed, Asgarli et al. show that any partition
P\in V(\mathcal{B}^{\kern 0.40974pt\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{
\hbox to6.07pt{\vbox to3.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.9pt\lower-1.64127pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 1.15 -1.99 4.75 -1.99 5.91 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\!(G))
with this property is equivalent to
P^{*}
up to automorphisms of
\mathcal{B}^{\kern 0.40974pt\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{
\hbox to6.07pt{\vbox to3.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.9pt\lower-1.64127pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 1.15 1.99 4.75 1.99 5.91 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 1.15 -1.99 4.75 -1.99 5.91 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 M 7.15 0 C 7.15 0.69 6.59 1.25 5.91 1.25 C 5.22 1.25 4.66 0.69 4.66 0 C 4.66 -0.69 5.22 -1.25 5.91 -1.25 C 6.59 -1.25 7.15 -0.69 7.15 0 Z M 5.91 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\!(G)
.

If
P^{*}\in V(\mathcal{B})
can be recognised, then it is not too difficult to recover
G^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{B}
(see Subsection
2.3
, observing that the arguments in this subsection can be simplified in this scenario).
However, it is not clear that there is any easy way to recognise
P^{*}
among the vertices of
\mathcal{B}
now that we no longer have multiple edges available to us.
Instead we will define a broader notion of a
P^{*}
-candidate
(Definition
2.1
), which can be recognised among the vertices of
\mathcal{B}
by direct inspection.
We will then show that there is at least one
P^{*}
-candidate in
\mathcal{B}
, and give an algorithm which reconstructs
G^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{B}
by using the local structure of
\mathcal{B}
near any
P^{*}
-candidate.
This will complete the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
Some extra work in Subsection
2.4
will then complete the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

We conclude this subsection by defining this notion of a
P^{*}
-candidate.
Let
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
.
As usual,
d(P)
denotes the degree of
P
in
\mathcal{B}
.
Define
N_{P}
to be the number of vertices plus the number of edges in
\mathcal{N}(P)
.
Define
T_{P}
to be the number of triangles
\Delta
in
\mathcal{N}(P)
such that the vertices of
\Delta
have a common neighbour in
\mathcal{B}
which does not lie in
N[P]
.

Definition 2.1
.

Call a partition
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
a
P^{*}
-candidate
if all of the following properties are satisfied.

•

Property
1
:
Any two neighbours of
P
which are not adjacent to each other have exactly one common neighbour
R
outside of
N[P]
, and no other neighbour of
P
is adjacent to
R
.

•

Property
2
:
Let
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\in N(P)
be neighbours of
P
such that
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}
is the vertex set of a triangle in
\mathcal{B}
, and such that
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
have a common neighbour
R
outside of
N[P]
.
Then every other neighbour of
P
is adjacent to either zero or two of
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
.

•

Property
3
:

d(P)\geq d(P^{\prime})
for every
P^{\prime}\in V(\mathcal{B})
which satisfies properties
1
and
2
.

•

Property
4
:

N_{P}\geq N_{P^{\prime}}
for every
P^{\prime}\in V(\mathcal{B})
which satisfies properties
1
,
2
, and
3
.

•

Property
5
:

T_{P}\geq T_{P^{\prime}}
for every
P^{\prime}\in V(\mathcal{B})
which satisfies properties
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
.

Observe that we can recognise the set of
P^{*}
-candidates among the vertices of
\mathcal{B}
by inspection.
We state here, without proof, that in the case where
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(G)
, it turns out that every
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
which satisfies properties
1
,
2
, and
3
also satisfies properties
4
and
5
.
However, we will not need this fact in our proofs.

2.2
The structure of partitions satisfying properties
1
,
2
, and
3

Our goal in this subsection is to prove Lemma
2.9
, which will show that
P^{*}
satisfies properties
1
,
2
, and
3
, and that if
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
is a partition satisfying properties
1
,
2
, and
3
, then
P
must have a very specific form.

We record the following useful observation.

Observation 2.2
.

Let
P
be a vertex of any induced subgraph
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{B}(G)
.
Let
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
be neighbours of
P
in
\mathcal{C}
, and let
v\in V(G)
.
Suppose
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
can each be obtained from
P
by moving
v
.
Then
Q_{2}
can be obtained from
Q_{1}
by moving
v
.

Lemma 2.3
.

P^{*}
satisfies properties
1
and
2
.

Proof.

Let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in N(P)
be non-adjacent.
Let
\psi(Q)=\{u,v\}
and
\psi(Q^{\prime})=\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
.
By Lemma
2.15
,
\psi(Q)
and
\psi(Q^{\prime})
are disjoint.
Let
R\notin N[P^{*}]
be a common neighbour of
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
.
Since
Q
is obtained from
P^{*}
by merging
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
,
R
is not obtained from
Q
by moving
u
or
v
by Observation
2.2
.
Hence
R(u)=R(v)
.
Hence
R
is the partition obtained from
Q^{\prime}
by merging
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
.
So
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
have a unique common neighbour
R\notin N[P]
.
Moreover, since every neighbour of
P^{*}
has exactly one part of size
2
and
n-2
parts of size
1
, any common neighbour of
R
and
P^{*}
is obtained from
R
by replacing either
\{u,v\}
with
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
or
\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
with
\{u^{\prime}\}
and
\{v^{\prime}\}
.
That is,
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are the only common neighbours of
P^{*}
and
R
.
Hence
P^{*}
satisfies property
1
.

Let
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\in N(P)
be such that
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}
is the vertex set of a triangle in
\mathcal{B}(G)
with some common neighbour
R\notin N[P]
.
Then by Lemma
2.19
,
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
form the three edges of a triangle in
\overline{G}
.
Let
Q\neq Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}
be a neighbour of
P
.
Then
\psi(Q)
is either disjoint from this triangle or is not, hence is incident with either zero or two edges of the triangle. Hence by Lemma
2.15
,
Q
is adjacent to either zero or two of
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
.
Hence
P^{*}
satisfies property
2
.
∎

For the rest of this subsection, let
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
be a fixed vertex of
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

If
P
has a part of size at least
4
, then
P
does not satisfy property
1
.

Proof.

Let
A
be a part of
P
of size at least
4
, and let
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
be distinct.
Let
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
be the partitions obtained from
P
by splitting
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
respectively.
If
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are adjacent, then
Q_{1}
is obtained from
Q_{2}
by splitting
a_{1}
since
\{a_{1}\}\in Q_{1}
and
\lvert Q_{2}(a_{1})\rvert\geq 3
.
But then
\{a_{2}\}\in Q_{1}
, a contradiction.
So
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are not adjacent.
Let
R_{1}
be the
a_{2}
-split neighbour of
Q_{1}
.
Then
R_{1}
is the
a_{1}
-split neighbour of
Q_{2}
.
Let
R_{2}
be obtained from
Q_{1}
by adding
a_{2}
to
\{a_{1}\}
.
Then
R_{2}
is obtained from
Q_{2}
by adding
a_{1}
to
\{a_{2}\}
.
So both
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are neighbours of both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
.
Observe that
A\backslash\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
is a part of both
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.
However, since
A
is a part of
P
of size at least
4
, we know that
A\backslash\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
has size at least
2
, and so is not a part of any neighbour of
P
.
So neither
R_{1}
nor
R_{2}
belong to
N[P]
.
So
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
have at least two common neighbours outside of
N[P]
.
So
P
does not satisfy property
1
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

If
P
has two distinct parts of size
2
with no edges of
G
between them, then
P
does not satisfy property
1
.

Proof.

Let
\{u,v\}
and
\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
be two parts of
P
of size
2
with no edges of
G
between them.
We will derive a contradiction by writing down partitions
Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\in V(\mathcal{B})
, which will be related to each other in
\mathcal{B}
as illustrated in Figure
1
.

Let
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
respectively be the neighbours of
P
obtained from
P
by adding
u^{\prime}
to
\{u,v\}
and by adding
u
to
\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
.
Observe that we have both
Q_{1}(u)=Q_{1}(v)
and
Q_{2}(u)\neq Q_{2}(v)
.
But
Q_{1}
is not obtained from
Q_{2}
by adding
u
to
Q_{2}(v)
nor by adding
v
to
Q_{2}(u)
.
Therefore,
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{B}
.
Let
R_{1}
be the partition obtained from
Q_{1}
by adding
v
to
\{v^{\prime}\}
.
Then
R_{1}
is obtained from
Q_{2}
by adding
v^{\prime}
to
\{v\}
.
Let
R_{2}
be the partition obtained from
Q_{1}
by adding
u
to
\{v^{\prime}\}
.
Then
R_{2}
is obtained from
Q_{2}
by adding
u^{\prime}
to
\{v\}
.
And
R_{1},R_{2}\notin N[P]
.
So
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
have two common neighbours outside of
N[P]
.
So
P
does not satisfy property
1
.
∎

u
v
u^{\prime}
v^{\prime}
P
u
v
u^{\prime}
v^{\prime}
Q_{1}
u
v^{\prime}
u^{\prime}
v
Q_{2}
u
v^{\prime}
u^{\prime}
v
R_{1}
u
u^{\prime}
v
v^{\prime}
R_{2}

Figure 1:
The situation in the proof of Lemma
2.5
.

Lemma 2.6
.

If
P
has a part of size
2
and a part of size
1
with no edges of
G
between them, then either
P
does not satisfy property
1
, or
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
with
\lvert P\rvert=k
.

Proof.

Suppose that either
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(G)
, or
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
with
\lvert P\rvert>k
.
Let
\{u,v\}
and
\{w\}
be two parts of
P
with no edge between them.
Let
Q_{1}
be the
u
-split neighbour of
P
.
Let
Q_{2}
be the partition obtained from
P
by adding
w
to
\{u,v\}
.
Then we have
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in N(P)
, where
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are not adjacent since
u
,
v
, and
w
each belong to distinct parts of
Q_{1}
, but all belong to the same part of
Q_{2}
.
Let
R
be a common neighbour of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
.
Then, since
R
is adjacent to
Q_{1}
, we know that
u
,
v
, and
w
do not all belong to the same part of
R
.
But since
R
is adjacent to
Q_{2}
, at least two vertices in
\{u,v,w\}
belong to the same part of
R
.
Hence
R
is obtained from
Q_{1}
by merging two of
\{u\}
,
\{v\}
, and
\{w\}
.
So either
R=P
, or
R
is obtained from
P
by adding either
u
or
v
to
\{w\}
.
So every common neighbour of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
lies in
N[P]
.
So
P
does not satisfy property
1
.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

If
P
has a part of size
3
and a part of size
1
with at least one non-edge of
G
between them, then
P
does not satisfy property
2
.

Proof.

Let
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}\in P
and
\{v\}\in P
be parts of
P
such that
u_{1}v\notin E(G)
. For each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, let
Q_{i}
be the
u_{i}
-split neighbour of
P
.
Let
R
be the partition obtained from
P
by replacing
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}
with
\{u_{1}\}
,
\{u_{2}\}
, and
\{u_{3}\}
.
Observe that for each distinct
i,j,k\in\{1,2,3\}
,
Q_{i}
is obtained from
Q_{j}
by adding
u_{k}
to
\{u_{j}\}
.
Moreover,
R
is the
u_{1}
-split neighbour of
Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
, and is the
u_{2}
-split neighbour of
Q_{1}
.
Therefore,
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},R\}
is the vertex set of a clique in
\mathcal{B}(G)
.
Moreover, we have
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\in N(P)
and
R\notin N[P]
, the latter since
u
,
v
, and
w
each belong to distinct parts of
R
but the same part of
P
.
Let
Q^{\prime}
be the partition obtained from
P
by adding
u_{1}
to
\{v\}
. Then we have
Q^{\prime}\in N(P)
, and
Q_{1}
is the
u_{1}
-split neighbour of
Q^{\prime}
.
But
Q^{\prime}
is not adjacent to
Q_{\ell}
for each
\ell\in\{2,3\}
, since
\{u_{1},u_{\ell}\}
is a part of
Q_{\ell}
, but
u_{1}
and
u_{\ell}
belong to distinct parts of size
2
in
Q^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

If
P
has a part
\{u,v\}
of size
2
and a part
\{w\}
of size
1
with exactly one edge of
G
between them, then either
w
has degree
n-2
, or
P
does not satisfy property
1
, or
P
does not satisfy property
2
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let
uw
be an edge and
vw
a non-edge of
G
.
Let
Q_{1}
be
u
-split neighbour of
P
. Let
Q_{2}
be the partition obtained from
P
by adding
v
to
\{w\}
.
Suppose
w
does not have degree
n-2
.
Then
w
has at least one non-neighbour
x\neq v
.
If
x
belongs to a part of
P
of size at least
4
, then by Lemma
2.4
,
P
does not satisfy property
1
.
If
x
belongs to a part of
P
of size
3
, then by Lemma
2.7
,
P
does not satisfy property
2
.

Suppose that
x
belongs to a part
\{x,y\}\in P
of size
2
, and that
vx\notin E(G)
.
Let
Q_{3}
be the
x
-split neighbour of
P
.
Let
R_{1}
be
x
-split neighbour of
Q_{1}
(observe that
R_{1}
is also the
u
-split neighbour of
Q_{3}
).
Let
R_{2}
be the partition obtained from
Q_{1}
by adding
x
to
\{v\}
(observe that
R_{2}
is also obtained from
Q_{3}
by adding
v
to
\{x\}
).
The situation is illustrated in Figure
2
.
By construction, we have
Q_{1},Q_{3}\in N(P)
, and both
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are adjacent to both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{3}
.
Moreover,
Q_{1}
and
Q_{3}
are not adjacent since
\{u\},\{v\}\in Q_{1}
and
\{u,v\}\in Q_{3}
, but
Q_{1}
is not the
u
-split neighbour of
Q_{3}
.
We have
R_{1}\notin N[P]
, since
\{x,y\}\in R_{1}
but
x
and
y
belong to distinct parts of
P
, each of size
2
.
Finally, we have
R_{2}\notin N[P]
because
\{u\},\{v\}\in R_{2}
and
\{u,v\}\in P
, but
R_{2}
is not the
u
-split neighbour of
P
.
Therefore,
P
does not satisfy property
1
.

Suppose that
x
belongs to a part
\{x,y\}\in P
of size
2
, and that
vx\in E(G)
.
Let
Q_{4}
be the partition obtained from
P
by adding
x
to
\{w\}
.
The situation is illustrated in Figure
3
.
Then
Q_{2},Q_{4}\in N(P)
, and
Q_{2}
and
Q_{4}
are not adjacent since
\{v,w\}\in Q_{2}
, but
v
and
w
belong to distinct parts of
Q_{4}
, each of size
2
.
Suppose there is some
R\notin N[P]
adjacent to both
Q_{2}
and
Q_{4}
.
Since
\{u,v\},\{w,x\}\in Q_{4}
and
uw,vx\in E(G)
, we know that
v
and
w
belong to different parts of
R
.
Therefore, since
\{v,w\}\in Q_{2}
, we know that
R
is obtained from
Q_{2}
by moving either
v
or
w
.
In particular, this means that either
\{v\}\in R
or
\{w\}\in R
, and that
R(x)=R(y)
.
The latter fact means
R
is obtained from
Q_{4}
by adding
y
to
\{w,x\}
(since adding
x
to
\{y\}
gives
P
), a contradiction as then neither
\{v\}
nor
\{w\}
are parts of
R
.
So no such
R
exists.
Therefore,
P
does not satisfy property
1
.

Suppose that
x
belongs to a part of
P
of size
1
.
Let
Q_{5}
be the partition obtained from
P
by adding
w
to
\{x\}
.
Let
R
be
u
-split neighbour of
Q_{5}
.
Then
R
is the
w
-split neighbour of
Q_{1}
, and
R
is obtained from
Q_{2}
by adding
w
to
\{x\}
.
But
R\notin N[P]
, since we have both
\{u\},\{v\}\in R
and
\{u,v\}\in P
, but
R
is not the
u
-split neighbour of
P
.
Similarly,
Q_{1}
is not adjacent to
Q_{5}
, since we have both
\{u\},\{v\}\in Q_{1}
and
\{u,v\}\in Q_{5}
, but
Q_{1}
is not the
u
-split neighbour of
Q_{5}
.
So
Q_{1}
and
Q_{5}
are neighbours of
P
which are not adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, but have a common neighbour outside of
N[P]
which is adjacent to another neighbour
Q_{2}
of
P
.
Therefore,
P
does not satisfy property
1
.
∎

.
