.

We are now ready to prove the lemma discussed at the start of this subsection.

Lemma 2.9
.

The following statements both hold.

1.

P^{*}
satisfies properties
1
,
2
, and
3
.

2.

Suppose
P
satisfies properties
1
,
2
, and
3
.
Then each of the following holds.

•

P
has no parts of size greater than
3
.

•

Every vertex in every part of size
3
has degree
n-3
in
G
.

•

If
A,B\in P
are distinct parts of size
2
, then every vertex in
A
is a neighbour of every vertex in
B
.

•

If
\{u,v\}\in P
is a part of size
2
and
\{w\}\in P
is a part of size
1
, then at least one of the following holds.

–

w
is a neighbour of both
u
and
v
, or

–

w
has degree
n-2
in
G
, or

–

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert=k
.

Proof.

We will prove both statements simultaneously.
Let
\Omega_{12}\subseteq V(\mathcal{B})
denote the set of vertices of
\mathcal{B}
which satisfy properties
1
and
2
.
Let
\Omega_{3}\subseteq\Omega_{12}
denote the set of vertices of
\mathcal{B}
which satisfy properties
1
,
2
, and
3
.
By definition,
\Omega_{3}
is just the set of vertices in
\Omega_{12}
which have maximal degree in
\mathcal{B}
.
Observe that
\Omega_{12}\neq\emptyset
, since
P^{*}\in\Omega_{12}
by Lemma
2.3
.
Let
P\in\Omega_{12}
be given.
It then suffices to show that
d(P)\leq d(P^{*})
, and that if
d(P)=d(P^{*})
, then each of the properties claimed in the statement of the lemma holds for
P
.

In order to relate the degrees of
P
and
P^{*}
, we define two functions
f_{P},f_{P^{*}}:P\times P\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.
Let
P^{\prime}\in\{P,P^{*}\}
and
A,B\in P
be given.
If
A\neq B
and
\lvert A\rvert=\lvert B\rvert=1
, then set
f_{P^{\prime}}(A,B)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}
if there is no edge between
A
and
B
, and set
f_{P^{\prime}}(A,B)\coloneqq 0
otherwise.
Otherwise, let
f_{P^{\prime}}(A,B)
be the number of neighbours
R\in N(P^{\prime})
which can be obtained from
P^{\prime}
by adding a vertex from
A
to a part contained in
B
(i.e. by moving a vertex
v\in V(G)
such that
P^{\prime}(v)\subseteq A
and
R(v)\subseteq B\cup\{v\}
).
Observe that
f(A,B)\neq f(B,A)
in general.

For each natural number
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P_{i}\subseteq P
denote the set of parts of
P
of size
i
.
By Lemma
2.4
, all parts of
P
have size at most
3
.
We can therefore write

\displaystyle d(P^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i=1}^{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in P_{i}\\
B\in P_{j}\end{subarray}}f_{P^{\prime}}(A,B).

(1)

We now compare the two functions
f_{P}
and
f_{P^{*}}
.

•

For each
A\in P_{1}
we have
f_{P}(A,A)=f_{P^{*}}(A,A)=0
.

•

For each
A\in P_{2}
we have
f_{P}(A,A)=f_{P^{*}}(A,A)=1
.

•

For each
A\in P_{3}
we have
f_{P}(A,A)=f_{P^{*}}(A,A)=3
.

•

Let
A,B\in P_{1}
be distinct.
Then, if there is an edge between
A
and
B
, we have
f_{P}(A,B)=f_{P^{*}}(A,B)=0
.
If there is no edge between
A
and
B
, then we have
f_{P^{*}}(A,B)=1
, and we have
f_{P}(A,B)\leq 1
by definition of
f_{P}
.
So in particular, we have

\displaystyle f_{P}(A,B)\leq f_{P^{*}}(A,B).

•

By Lemma
2.6
, for each
A\in P_{1}
and
B\in P_{2}
we have
f_{P}(A,B)=f_{P^{*}}(A,B)=0
, and either there is one non-edge between
A
and
B
, in which case
f_{P}(B,A)=f_{P^{*}}(B,A)=1
, or there are no non-edges between
A
and
B
, in which case
f_{P}(B,A)=f_{P^{*}}(B,A)=0
.

•

By Lemma
2.7
, for each
A\in P_{1}
,
B\in P_{3}
, we have
f_{P}(A,B)=f_{P^{*}}(A,B)=f_{P}(B,A)=f_{P^{*}}(B,A)=0
.

•

Let
A,B\in P_{2}\cup P_{3}
be distinct.
Observe that
f_{P}(A,B)+f_{P}(B,A)
equals the number of vertices in
A
with no neighbours in
B
plus the number of vertices in
B
with no neighbours in
A
, while
f_{P^{*}}(A,B)+f_{P^{*}}(B,A)
equals the number of non-edges from
A
to
B
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle f_{P}(A,B)+f_{P}(B,A)\leq f_{P^{*}}(A,B)+f_{P^{*}}(B,A),

(2)

with equality if and only if either there are no non-edges between
A
and
B
, or we have
\lvert A\rvert=\lvert B\rvert=2
and there are no edges between
A
and
B
.

Therefore, by (
1
), we have
d(P)\leq d(P^{*})
as claimed.
Moreover, if
d(P)=d(P^{*})
, then by the condition given to achieve equality in (
2
), together with Lemmas
2.4
,
2.5
,
2.6
,
2.7
, and
2.8
,
P
satisfies every condition claimed in the statement of the lemma.
∎

2.3
The structure of
\mathcal{B}
near vertices satisfying properties
3
,
4
, and
5

For the rest of this section, let
\Omega_{3}\subseteq V(\mathcal{B})
denote the set of vertices satisfying properties
1
,
2
and
3
, let
\Omega_{4}\subseteq\Omega_{3}
denote the set of vertices of
\Omega_{3}
which also satisfy property
4
, and let
\Omega_{5}\subseteq\Omega_{4}
denote the set of vertices of
\Omega_{4}
which also satisfy property
5
.
Observe that, by definition,
\Omega_{5}
is the set of
P^{*}
-candidates in
\mathcal{B}
.
Lemma
2.9
tells us that
P^{*}\in\Omega_{3}
, and that each partition
P\in\Omega_{3}
is forced to have a very specific form.
In this subsection, we will study the local structure of
\mathcal{B}
near each
P\in\Omega_{3}
, with the ultimate goal of showing that we can use the local structure of
\mathcal{B}
near any
P^{*}
-candidate (i.e. near any
P\in\Omega_{5}
) to determine the graph
G^{\prime}
.

We begin with the following classical theorem of Whitney
[
14
]
.
As usual, the
line graph

L(H)
of a graph
H
is the graph with vertex set
E(H)
where
e,f\in V(L(H))
are adjacent if and only if
e
and
f
are incident in
H
, and the
claw

K_{1,3}
is the graph with one vertex of degree
3
and three vertices of degree
1
.

Theorem 2.10

(Whitney, 1932
[
14
]
)
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be connected graphs not isomorphic to the claw
K_{1,3}
.
If
L(G_{1})\cong L(G_{2})
, then
G_{1}\cong G_{2}
.

Observing that the disjoint union
G_{1}+G_{2}
of two graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
satisfies
L(G_{1}+G_{2})=L(G_{1})+L(G_{2})
, that
L(K_{1,3})\cong L(K_{3})
, and that
L(K_{1})
is the graph on no vertices, Whitney’s Theorem says that we can ‘almost’ determine any graph
H
from its line graph
L(H)
, except that we cannot distinguish components of
H
isomorphic to the claw
K_{1,3}
from those isomorphic to the triangle
K_{3}
, and that we may miss isolated vertices of
H
.
We therefore obtain the following, which we will use at the end of this subsection.

Observation 2.11
.

Let
H
be a graph.
Let
H^{\ddagger}
be the graph obtained from
H
by removing all of its isolated vertices, and by replacing all of its triangle components with claw components.
Then
H^{\ddagger}
can be determined from
L(H)
.

An outline of what we will show in this subsection is as follows.
First, we will show that the open neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}(P^{*})
is isomorphic to the line graph
L(\overline{G})
, and that for each
P\in\Omega_{3}
, the open neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}(P)
is isomorphic to a subgraph of
L(\overline{G})
.
Recall that, for each
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
, we use
N_{P}
to denote the number of vertices plus the number of edges in
\mathcal{N}(P)
, and that
\Omega_{4}\subseteq\Omega_{3}
is the set of
P\in\Omega_{3}
such that
N_{P}\geq N_{P^{\prime}}
for all
P^{\prime}\in\Omega_{3}
.
Therefore we have
P^{*}\in\Omega_{4}
, and for each
P\in\Omega_{4}
we have
\mathcal{N}(P)\cong L(\overline{G})
.
Hence, by Whitney’s Theorem
2.10
, we can almost determine
G^{\prime}
from any
P\in\Omega_{4}
, except that we do not yet have a way to distinguish between triangles and claws in
\overline{G}
.
Fix any
P\in\Omega_{4}
.
Then each triangle in
L(\overline{G})\cong\mathcal{N}(P)
corresponds to either a triangle or a claw in
\overline{G}
.
We will show the following.

1.

For each triangle
\Delta
in
\mathcal{N}(P)
, if
\Delta
arose from a claw in
\overline{G}
, then every common neighbour of the three vertices in
\Delta
lies in
N[P]
.

2.

For each triangle
\Delta^{*}
in
\mathcal{N}(P^{*})
, if
\Delta^{*}
arose from a triangle in
\overline{G}
, then the three vertices in
\Delta^{*}
have a common neighbour
R\notin N[P^{*}]
.

Recall that, for each
P^{\prime}\in V(\mathcal{B})
, we use
T_{P^{\prime}}
to denote the number of triangles in
\mathcal{N}(P^{\prime})
which have a common neighbour
R\notin N[P^{\prime}]
, and that
\Omega_{5}\subseteq\Omega_{4}
is the set of vertices
P\in\Omega_{4}
such that
T_{P}\geq T_{P^{\prime}}
for every
P^{\prime}\in\Omega_{4}
.
Therefore, for each
P\in\Omega_{5}
, we know that each triangle
\Delta
in
\mathcal{N}(P)
arose from a triangle in
\overline{G}
if and only if the vertices of
\Delta
have a common neighbour outside of
N[P]
, and arose from a claw otherwise.
Then we can reconstruct
G^{\prime}
from the local structure of
\mathcal{B}
near any
P\in\Omega_{5}
, as required.

We begin by defining the following useful function, which associates each neighbour of a vertex
P\in\Omega_{3}
with a non-edge
uv\in E(\overline{G})
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
P\in\Omega_{3}
.
Define the map
\psi_{P}:N(P)\rightarrow E(\overline{G})
for each
Q\in N(P)
as follows.

•

If
Q
is obtained from
P
by merging two parts
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
, let
\psi_{P}(Q)\coloneqq\{u,v\}
, and call
Q
a
type
1
neighbour of
P
.

•

If
Q
is obtained from
P
by replacing a part
\{u,v\}
with two parts
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
, let
\psi_{P}(Q)\coloneqq\{u,v\}
, and call
Q
a
type
2
neighbour of
P
.

•

If
Q
is obtained from
P
by replacing two parts
\{u,v\}
and
\{w\}
with two parts
\{u\}
and
\{v,w\}
, let
\psi_{P}(Q)\coloneqq\{v,w\}
, and call
Q
a
type
3
neighbour of
P
if
w
has degree
n-2
and call
Q
a
type
4
neighbour of
P
otherwise.

•

If
Q
is obtained from
P
by replacing a part
\{u,v,w\}
with two parts
\{u\}
and
\{v,w\}
(where
u,v
, and
w
all have degree
n-3
by Definition
2.1
), let
\psi_{P}(Q)\coloneqq\{v,w\}
, and call
Q
a
type
5
neighbour of
P
.

P
Q
type
1
P
Q
type
2
P
Q
type
3
or
4
P
Q
type
5

Figure 4:
The five types of neighbours
Q
of a
P^{*}
-candidate
P
.
For each type, the dashed line represents the non-edge
\psi_{P}(Q)\in E(\overline{G})
.

For each
Q\in N(P)
, let
Q)\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
represent the type of
Q
.
These five types of neighbour are illustrated in Figure
4
.
For ease of notation, we will define
\psi\coloneqq\psi_{P}
when the choice of
P
is clear from context.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
P\in\Omega_{3}
.
Then
\psi_{P}
is well-defined and injective.
Moreover,
\psi_{P^{*}}
is bijective.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.9
, every non-edge
uv\in E(\overline{G})
either lies within a part of
P
of size
3
or
2
, or between a part of
P
of size
2
and a part of
P
of size
1
, or between two parts of
P
of size
1
.
If
P=P^{*}
, then in addition, for each non-edge of
G
, there is exactly one
Q\in N(P)
such that
\psi_{P^{*}}(Q)=uv
.
∎

Given two neighbours
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
of a vertex
P\in\Omega_{3}
, the following lemma records the cases where it is possible that
\psi(Q)
and
\psi(Q^{\prime})
are incident as edges of
\overline{G}
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
P\in\Omega_{3}
, and let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in N(P)
be distinct neighbours of
P
, where
Q)\leq Q^{\prime})
.
If
\psi(Q)
and
\psi(Q^{\prime})
are incident (as edges of
\overline{G}
), then either
Q)=Q^{\prime})\in\{1,3,4,5\}
, or
Q)\in\{1,2\}
and
Q^{\prime})\in\{3,4\}
.

Proof.

Let
v\in\psi(Q)\cap\psi(Q^{\prime})
.
If
Q)=1
or
Q^{\prime})=1
then we have
\lvert P(v)\rvert=1
.
If
Q)=2
or
Q^{\prime})=2
then we have
\lvert P(v)\rvert=2
.
If
Q)\in\{3,4\}
or
Q^{\prime})\in\{3,4\}
then we have
\lvert P(v)\rvert\in\{1,2\}
.
Finally, if
Q)=5
or
Q^{\prime})=5
then we have
\lvert P(v)\rvert=3
.
Therefore, we know that either
Q)=Q^{\prime})
, or
Q)\in\{1,2\}
and
Q^{\prime})\in\{3,4\}
.
Moreover, if
Q)=Q^{\prime})=2
, then we have
\psi(Q),\psi(Q^{\prime})\in P
, so
\psi(Q)
and
\psi(Q^{\prime})
are disjoint, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
P\in\Omega_{3}
, and let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in N(P)
be distinct neighbours of
P
.
If
QQ^{\prime}\in E(\mathcal{B})
, then
\psi(Q)
and
\psi(Q^{\prime})
are incident as edges of
\overline{G}
.

Proof.

Since
\psi(Q)
and
\psi(Q^{\prime})
are not incident, by definition of
\psi
there are at least four vertices
v
such that
Q(v)\neq Q^{\prime}(v)
.
Suppose
QQ^{\prime}\in E(\mathcal{B})
.
Then
Q^{\prime}
must be obtained from
Q
by moving a vertex such that
\lvert Q(v)\cup Q^{\prime}(v)\rvert\geq 4
, i.e. such that
\lvert Q(v)\rvert+\lvert Q^{\prime}(v)\rvert\geq 5
.
But every part of
P
has size at most
3
, and there is at least one edge between each part of size
3
and every other part of
P
.
So every part of
Q
has size at most
3
.
Therefore, without loss of generality
\lvert Q(v)\rvert=3
and
\lvert Q^{\prime}(v)\rvert\in\{2,3\}
.
Hence
v
has degree at most
n-4
and belongs to a part of size
3
in
Q
.
But by definition of a
P^{*}
-candidate, it is not possible that
Q
is obtained from
P
by adding a vertex to a part of size
2
, since any vertex not adjacent to both vertices in a part of
P
of size
2
belongs to a part of
P
of size
1
, and if such a vertex exists, we have
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
with
\lvert P\rvert=k
, meaning such a neighbour would have
k-1
parts, a contradiction.
Therefore, since every part of
P
has size at most
3
, every part of size
3
in
Q
is a part of size
3
in
P
.
Therefore,
v
belongs to a part of size
3
in
P
.
Therefore
v
has degree
n-3
by definition of a
P^{*}
-candidate.
So
QQ^{\prime}\notin E(\mathcal{B})
.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

The following statements hold.

1.

We have
P^{*}\in\Omega_{4}
.

2.

Let
P\in\Omega_{4}
.
Let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in N(P)
be distinct neighbours of
P
.
Then
Q_{1}Q_{2}\in E(\mathcal{B})
if and only if
\psi(Q_{1})
and
\psi(Q_{2})
are incident as edges of
\overline{G}
.
Moreover,
\psi_{P}
is bijective, and
\mathcal{N}(P)
is isomorphic to the line graph
L(\overline{G})
.

Proof.

We will prove both statements simultaneously.
We first examine the neighbourhood of
P^{*}
.
Let
Q_{1}^{*},Q_{2}^{*}\in N(P^{*})
be distinct neighbours of
P^{*}
. Suppose that
\psi(Q_{1}^{*})
and
\psi(Q_{2}^{*})
are incident at a vertex
u\in V(G)
.
Then
Q_{1}^{*}
is obtained from
P^{*}
by merging
\{u\}
with a part
\{v_{1}\}\in P^{*}
, and
Q_{2}^{*}
is obtained from
P^{*}
by merging
\{u\}
with a part 8
\{v_{2}\}\in P^{*}
.
Then
Q_{2}^{*}
can be obtained from
Q_{1}^{*}
by adding
u
to
\{v_{2}\}
.
So
Q_{1}^{*}Q_{2}^{*}\in E(\mathcal{B})
.
Conversely, since
P^{*}\in\Omega_{3}
by Lemma
2.9
, we know that if
Q_{1}^{*}Q_{2}^{*}\in E(\mathcal{B})
, then
\psi(Q_{1}^{*})
and
\psi(Q_{2}^{*})
are incident.
Since
\psi_{P^{*}}
is bijective by Lemma
2.13
, it follows that
\mathcal{N}(P^{*})\cong L(\overline{G})
by definition of a line graph.

Now, recall that for each
P^{\prime}\in V(\mathcal{B})
, we define
N_{P^{\prime}}
to be the number of vertices plus the number of edges in
\mathcal{N}(P^{\prime})
, and recall that
\Omega_{4}\subseteq\Omega_{3}
is the set of vertices
P^{\prime}\in\Omega_{3}
such that
N_{P^{\prime}}\geq N_{P^{\prime\prime}}
for all
P^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega_{3}
.
Let
P\in\Omega_{3}
be given, and let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in N(P)
be distinct neighbours of
P
.
By Lemma
2.15
we know that if
Q_{1}Q_{2}\in E(\mathcal{B})
, then
\psi(Q_{1})
and
\psi(Q_{2})
are incident.
Therefore, since
\psi_{P}
is injective by Lemma
2.13
, we know that
\mathcal{N}(P)
is isomorphic to a subgraph of the line graph
L(\overline{G})
.
It follows that
N_{P}\leq N_{P^{*}}
, which means that
P^{*}\in\Omega_{4}
since
P
was an arbitrary element of
\Omega_{3}
.
Moreover, if
\psi_{P}
is not surjective, or if
\psi(Q_{1})
and
\psi(Q_{2})
are incident and
Q_{1}Q_{2}\notin E(\mathcal{B})
, then
\mathcal{N}(P)
is isomorphic to a proper subgraph of
L(\overline{G})
.
Since
\mathcal{N}(P^{*})\cong L(\overline{G})
, this means that
N_{P}<N_{P^{*}}
, which means that
P\notin\Omega_{4}
.
So for every
P\in\Omega_{4}
, we know that
\psi_{P}
is bijective, and that if
\psi(Q_{1})
and
\psi(Q_{2})
are incident, then
Q_{1}Q_{2}\in E(\mathcal{B})
.
In particular,
\mathcal{N}(P)\cong L(\overline{G})
for every
P\in\Omega_{4}
by definition of a line graph.
∎

We now rule out a case where certain vertices
P\in\Omega_{3}
do not satisfy
\mathcal{N}(P)\cong L(\overline{G})
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
P\in\Omega_{3}
, and let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in N(P)
be neighbours of
P
.
If
\psi(Q_{1})
and
\psi(Q_{2})
are incident and
Q_{1})=1
and
Q_{2})\in\{3,4\}
, then we have
P\notin\Omega_{4}
.

Proof.

We claim that
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{B}
.
In this case, we know that
\mathcal{N}(P)
is a proper subgraph of
L(\overline{G})
by Lemma
2.15
, so
P\notin\Omega_{4}
by Lemma
2.16
.

It remains to prove the claim.
Let
u
denote the common vertex of
\psi(Q_{1})
and
\psi(Q_{2})
.
Then
Q_{1}
is obtained from
P
by merging the part
\{u\}\in P
with another part
\{v\}\in P
, and
Q_{2}
is obtained from
P
by adding a vertex
w
from a part
\{w,x\}\in P
to
\{u\}
.
Then we have
\{u,v\},\{w,x\}\in Q_{1}
, while
\{u,w\}\in Q_{2}
.
So
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{B}
.
∎

Lemma
2.16
allows us to determine the line graph
L(\overline{G})
from
\mathcal{B}
.
It remains to show that we can distinguish between claws and triangles in
\overline{G^{\prime}}
.
In order to do this, we will examine the neighbourhood of the vertices
P\in\Omega_{4}
at distance
2
from
P
, as discussed in the outline at the beginning of this subsection.
We begin by recording the following useful observation, which in this section we will apply to the case where
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{B}
.

Observation 2.18
.

Let
P
be a vertex of any subgraph
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{B}(G)
.
Let
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
be neighbours of
P
in
\mathcal{C}
.
If
Q_{1}
is obtained from
P
by moving some vertex
v
such that
Q_{1}(v)=Q_{2}(v)
, then we have
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
P\in\Omega_{4}
.
Let
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}\subseteq N(P)
be the vertex set of a triangle in
\mathcal{B}
.
If
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
are the three edges of a claw in
\overline{G}
, then
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
do not have any common neighbour outside of
N[P]
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
Q_{1})\leq Q_{2})\leq Q_{3})
.

By Lemma
2.17
, if we have
Q_{1})=1
and either
Q_{2})=4
or
Q_{3})=4
, then we know that
P\notin\Omega_{4}
, a contradiction.
If we have
Q_{1})=Q_{2})=Q_{3})=5
, then by definition,
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
are the three edges of a triangle in
\overline{G}
, a contradiction.

Therefore, by Lemma
2.14
, we need only consider the following two cases.

Case
1
:
Suppose that
Q_{1})=Q_{2})=Q_{3})=1
.
Let
x,q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\in V(G)
be vertices such that
\psi(Q_{1})=\{x,q_{1}\}
,
\psi(Q_{2})=\{x,q_{2}\}
, and
\psi(Q_{3})=\{x,q_{3}\}
.
Let
R\notin N[P]
be adjacent to
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
where
R
is obtained from
Q_{1}
by moving some vertex
a\in V(G)
.
Observe that
Q_{1}(b)=Q_{2}(b)
for all
b\in V(G)\backslash\{x,q_{1},q_{2}\}
and that
Q_{1}(c)=Q_{3}(c)
for all
c\in V(G)\backslash\{x,q_{1},q_{3}\}
.
Therefore, we have
a\in\{x,q_{1},q_{2}\}\cap\{x,q_{1},q_{3}\}=\{x,q_{1}\}
by Observation
2.18
.
But since
R\notin N[P]
, we have
a\notin\{x,q_{1}\}
by Observation
2.2
, a contradiction.
So no such
R
exists.

Case
2
:
Suppose
Q_{1})\in\{2,3,4\}
and
Q_{2}),Q_{3})\in\{3,4\}
.
Then
Q_{2}
is obtained from
P
by adding a vertex
u_{2}
from a part
\{u_{2},v_{2}\}\in P
to a part
\{w_{2}\}\in P
, so that
\psi(Q_{2})=\{u_{2},w_{2}\}
.
Let
Q^{\prime}\in N(P)\cap N(Q_{2})
be any common neighbour of
P
and
Q_{2}
such that
Q^{\prime})\in\{3,4\}
.
Then
Q^{\prime}
is obtained from
P
by adding a vertex
u^{\prime}
from a part
\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}\in P
to a part
\{w^{\prime}\}\in P
, so that
\psi(Q^{\prime})=\{u^{\prime},w^{\prime}\}
.
By Lemma
2.15
,
\psi(Q^{\prime})
shares a vertex with
\psi(Q_{2})
, so either
u_{2}=u^{\prime}
or
w_{2}=w^{\prime}
.
Suppose that
w_{2}=w^{\prime}
and
\psi_{P}(Q^{\prime})\neq\{v_{2},w_{2}\}
.
Then
\{u_{2},w_{2}\}
is a part of
Q_{2}
, whereas
u_{2}
and
w_{2}
belong to distinct parts of
Q^{\prime}
, each of size
2
.
So
Q_{2}
is not adjacent to
Q^{\prime}
, a contradiction.
So either
u_{2}=u^{\prime}
, or both
w_{2}=w^{\prime}
and
\psi_{P}(Q^{\prime})=\{v_{2},w_{2}\}
.
Since
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
are distinct, this means that if
w_{2}
is the common vertex of
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
, then we cannot have both
Q_{1}),Q_{3})\in\{3,4\}
.
Therefore we have
Q_{1})=2
in this case, a contradiction as
\{w_{2}\}\in P
is a part of size
1
.
So
u_{2}
is the common vertex of
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
.
So
Q_{3}
is obtained from
P
by adding
u_{2}
to some part
\{w_{3}\}\in P
, where
w_{3}\neq w_{2}
.

Now suppose
R\notin N[P]
is a common neighbour of
Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
.
Since
R\notin N[P]
,
R
is not obtained from
Q_{2}
or
Q_{3}
by moving
u_{2}
by Observation
2.2
.
If any of
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, or
Q_{3}
have type
4
, then
\lvert P\rvert=k
.
Otherwise,
Q_{2})=Q_{3})=3
, so
w_{2}
and
w_{3}
each have degree
n-2
in
G
, and so are adjacent to each other in
G
.
So in either case,
R
is not obtained from
Q_{2}
by adding
w_{3}
to
\{u_{2},w_{2}\}
, and
R
is not obtained from
Q_{3}
by adding
w_{2}
to
\{u_{2},w_{3}\}
.
So
R(w_{2})\neq R(u_{2})
and
R(w_{3})\neq R(u_{2})
.
So
R
is obtained from
Q_{2}
by moving
w_{2}
, and
R
is obtained from
Q_{3}
by moving
w_{3}
.
The former statement means that
\{w_{3}\}\in R
, so by the latter statement,
R
is obtained from
Q_{3}
by splitting
w_{3}
.
But this is a contradiction, since then
R
is obtained from
P
by splitting
u_{2}
, meaning
R\in N[P]
.
So no such
R
exists.
∎

So far, every proof in this section has held for
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}(G),\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)\}
, where
k\leq n-1
.
However, for the rest of this section, we will further suppose that
k\leq n-2
.

Lemma 2.20
.

Suppose
k\leq n-2
.
Let
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}\subseteq N(P^{*})
be the vertex set of a triangle in
\mathcal{B}
.
If
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
are the three edges of a triangle in
\overline{G}
, then
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
have a common neighbour
R\notin N[P^{*}]
.

Proof.

If
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
form the three edges of a triangle, then let
\{u,v,w\}
be the vertex set of this triangle, and let
R
be the partition obtained from
P^{*}
by replacing
\{u\}
,
\{v\}
, and
\{w\}
with
\{u,v,w\}
.
Then
R
is a neighbour of
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
, and
R\notin N[P^{*}]
.
∎

The following lemma completes the proof of Theorem
1.4
, from which Theorem
1.1
follows immediately.

Lemma 2.21
.

Suppose
k\leq n-2
.
Then we can determine
G^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Recall that, for each
P\in\Omega_{4}
, we define
T_{P}
to be the number of triangles
\Delta
in
\mathcal{N}(P)
such that the vertices of
\Delta
have a common neighbour in
\mathcal{B}
which does not lie in
N[P]
.
Recall that we define
\Omega_{5}\subseteq\Omega_{4}
to be the set of
P\in\Omega_{4}
such that
T_{P}\geq T_{P^{\prime}}
for all
P^{\prime}\in\Omega_{4}
.

Since
P^{*}\in\Omega_{4}
by Lemma
2.16
, we know that
\Omega_{4}
is non-empty.
Therefore
\Omega_{5}
is non-empty by definition of
\Omega_{5}
.
Observe that we can recognise the vertices in
\Omega_{5}
by direct inspection of
\mathcal{B}
.
Let
P\in\Omega_{5}
be given.
Since
\Omega_{5}\subseteq\Omega_{4}
, by Lemma
2.16
we know that
\mathcal{N}(P)
is isomorphic to the line graph
L(\overline{G})
.
By Observation
2.11
, we can therefore recognise the graph
\overline{G}^{\ddagger}
obtained from
\overline{G}
by removing all of its isolated vertices and by replacing every triangle component with a claw component.
Let
T
denote the number of triangles in
\overline{G}
, and let
T^{\ddagger}
denote the number of triangles in
\overline{G}^{\ddagger}
.
Observe that
\overline{G^{\prime}}
is the graph obtained from
\overline{G}
by removing all of its isolated vertices, since a vertex is universal in
G
if and only if it is isolated in
\overline{G}
.
Therefore,
\overline{G^{\prime}}
is the graph obtained from
\overline{G}^{\ddagger}
by replacing
T-T^{\ddagger}
of its claw components with triangle components.
Clearly we can determine
T^{\ddagger}
from
\overline{G}^{\ddagger}
.
Suppose that we also have a strategy to determine
T
.
Then we can determine
\overline{G^{\prime}}
, and therefore we can determine
G^{\prime}
, completing the proof.

It remains to show that we can determine
T
.
Let
P\in\Omega_{4}
be fixed.
We claim that if
P\in\Omega_{5}
, then
T_{P}=T
.
If so, we are done since we can recognise
\Omega_{5}
, and since we can determine
T_{P^{\prime}}
from
\mathcal{B}
for each
P^{\prime}\in\Omega_{5}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{P}
denote the set of triangles in
\mathcal{N}(P)
.
Let
X_{T}
denote the set of triangles in
\overline{G}
, let
X_{C}
denote the set of claws in
\overline{G}
, and let
X\coloneqq X_{T}\cup X_{C}
.
By Lemma
2.16
, for each triangle or claw
K\in X
with edges
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
, and
e_{3}
, there exist distinct neighbours
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
of
P
such that
\psi(Q_{i})=e_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and such that
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}
is the vertex set of a triangle in
\mathcal{N}(P)
.
On the other hand, let
\Delta\in\mathcal{T}_{P}
be a triangle on vertex set
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}
.
Then, again by Lemma
2.16
, we know that
\psi_{P}(Q_{1})
,
\psi_{P}(Q_{2})
, and
\psi_{P}(Q_{3})
are pairwise incident as edges of
\overline{G}
, and therefore are either the three edges of a triangle in
\overline{G}
or the three edges of a claw in
\overline{G}
.
Therefore, there is a bijection
\xi:\mathcal{T}_{P}\rightarrow X
which sends each triangle
\Delta
in
\mathcal{N}(P)
on vertex set
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
, and
Q_{3}
to the triangle or claw in
\overline{G}
with edges
\psi(Q_{1})
,
\psi(Q_{2})
, and
\psi(Q_{3})
.
Moreover, Lemma
2.19
tells us that if
\xi(\Delta)\in X_{C}
, then the three vertices of
\Delta
have no common neighbour outside of
N[P]
.
So therefore
T_{P}\leq\lvert X_{T}\rvert=T
.
On the other hand, in the special case where
P=P^{*}
, Lemma
2.20
tells us that if
\xi(\Delta)\in X_{T}
, then the three vertices of
\Delta
do have a common neighbour outside of
N[P]
.
So we have
T_{P^{*}}\geq\lvert X_{T}\rvert=T
, which means that
T_{P^{*}}=T
.
By definition of
\Omega_{5}
, this means that
T_{P}=T
for every
P\in\Omega_{5}
, as claimed.
∎

2.4
Completing the proof of Theorem
1.5

In this brief subsection, we will complete the proof of Theorem
1.5
, from which Theorem
1.3
immediately follows.
Most of the work has already been completed.
It remains to examine how the upper-Bell
k
-colouring graphs
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
change as we vary
k
, or remove universal vertices from
G
.

The following observation follows immediately from our previous observation that in any independent set partition of any graph, any universal vertex must belong to a part of size
1
.

Observation 2.22
.

Let
G^{+}
be the graph obtained from
G
by adding a universal vertex. Then
\chi(G^{+})=\chi(G)+1
and
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k+1}(G^{+})
.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
be natural numbers. If
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G)\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G)
, then either
k_{i}>n
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
, or
\chi(G)<k_{i}\leq n
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
and
k_{1}=k_{2}
, or
k_{i}\leq\chi(G)
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

For each natural number
k
, there exists a partition of
V(G)
into exactly
k
independent sets if and only if
k\in[\chi(G),n]
.
Hence
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)=\mathcal{B}_{\geq\chi(G)}(G)
if
k\leq\chi(G)
, and
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)=\mathcal{B}_{\geq n+1}(G)
if
k>n
, and every graph in
\{\mathcal{B}_{\geq\chi(G)}(G),\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{\geq n+1}(G)\}
is distinct as these graphs each have a different number of vertices to each other.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be graphs on
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
vertices respectively.
Let
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
be natural numbers.
Suppose
n_{1}-k_{1}=n_{2}-k_{2}
and
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
.
Then
\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{1}}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{2}}}(G_{2})
.

Proof.

Let
n^{\prime}
denote the number of vertices of
G_{1}^{\prime}
(or
G_{2}^{\prime}
).
For each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
G_{i}
is obtained from
G_{i}^{\prime}
by adding
n_{i}-n^{\prime}
universal vertices.
Hence, by Observation
2.22
, we have
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}-(n_{1}-n^{\prime})}(G_{i}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{i}}(G_{i})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Moreover, since
n_{1}-k_{1}=n_{2}-k_{2}
, we know that
k_{1}-(n_{1}-n^{\prime})=k_{2}-(n_{2}-n^{\prime})
, and therefore we have
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}-n_{1}+n^{\prime}}(G_{1}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}-n_{2}+n^{\prime}}(G_{2}^{\prime})
.
Therefore we have
\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{1}}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{2}}}(G_{2})
, as claimed.
∎

We now complete the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

For the ‘if’ direction, suppose
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
.
If
n_{1}-k_{1}=n_{2}-k_{2}
, then we have
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
by Lemma
2.24
and we are done.
If
k_{i}\leq\chi(G_{i})
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
, then
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{i}}(G_{i})=\mathcal{B}(G_{i})
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.
But we know that
\mathcal{B}(G_{1}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{B}(G_{2}^{\prime})
, so we have
\mathcal{B}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}(G_{2})
by Theorem
1.4
.
Therefore, we have
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
.

For the ‘only if’ direction, if
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
, we have
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
by Lemma
2.21
.
Let
n^{\prime}
denote the number of vertices of
G_{1}^{\prime}
(or
G_{2}^{\prime}
).
Since we have
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
, we have

\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}-(n_{1}-n^{\prime})}(G_{1}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}-(n_{2}-n^{\prime})}(G_{2}^{\prime})
by Observation
2.22
.
Since
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
, Lemma
2.23
guarantees that one of the following three scenarios holds.
Either we have
k_{i}-(n_{i}-n^{\prime})>n_{i}
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
, which means that
k_{i}>n_{i}
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
, a contradiction.
Or, we have
k_{1}-(n_{1}-n^{\prime})=k_{2}-(n_{2}-n^{\prime})
, which means that
n_{1}-k_{1}=n_{2}-k_{2}
, as claimed.
Or, we have
k_{i}-(n_{i}-n^{\prime})\leq\chi(G_{i}^{\prime})
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
, which means
k_{i}\leq\chi(G_{i})
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
by Observation
2.22
, as claimed.
∎

3
Proof of Theorem
1.2

In this section we will prove Theorem
1.2
.
Fix a graph
G
on
n
vertices and a natural number
k
.
We use the same terminology introduced at the start of Section
2
. Our proof will not use any results from Section
2
(except for Observations
2.18
and
2.2
), instead expanding on ideas from Asgarli et al.
[
2
]
, Berthe et al.
[
4
]
, and Hogan et al.
[
11
]
, where the local structure of a recolouring graph
\mathcal{C}_{k}(G)
is used to determine the underlying graph
G
.
Unfortunately, the local structure of a Bell
k
-colouring graph is considerably more complex.
In the case of recolouring graphs, it is straightforward to show that the neighbourhood of any partition
P
consists of disjoint cliques, each corresponding to recolouring a given vertex of the underlying graph.
However this is not necessarily true for Bell colouring graphs if
P
contains parts of size less than
4
.
In particular, it is true that any two partitions obtained from
P
by moving the same vertex of
G
are adjacent to each other (this is Observation
2.2
), however observe that if
\{u,v\}\in P
has size
2
, then the
u
-split neighbour and the
v
-split neighbour of
P
are equal, and that if
\{u,v,w\}\in P
is a part of size
3
, then the
u
-split,
v
-split, and
w
-split neighbours of
P
are each adjacent to each other.
Nonetheless, if we can guarantee the existence of a partition
P^{\dagger}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
where every part of
P^{\dagger}
has size at least
4
, then so long as
\lvert P^{\dagger}\rvert<k
, it will be possible with some care to use a similar approach to Berthe et al. to recover the underlying graph
G
.

In particular, our approach for proving Theorem
1.2
is as follows.
For the rest of this section, suppose
G
has maximum degree
\Delta(G)<\frac{1}{9}n-\frac{1}{3}
, and suppose
k\geq\chi(G)+1
.
We will show that this maximum degree condition on
G
guarantees the existence of a partition
P^{\dagger}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
such that
\lvert P^{\dagger}\rvert=\chi(G)
and every part of
P^{\dagger}
has size at least
4
(see Lemma
3.1
).
By Observation
2.2
, for every partition
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
, the open neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}(P)
has at most
n
components, each corresponding to at least one vertex of
G
.
In particular, we will show that
\mathcal{N}(P^{\dagger})
has exactly
n
components, each corresponding to a unique vertex of
G
.
Then, looking only at partitions
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
whose neighbourhoods contain a maximal number of components (i.e.
n
components), we will construct a candidate graph
G_{P}
for each such
P
by looking at the neighbourhood in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
at distance
2
from
P
. (Note that the method for constructing these candidate graphs will differ depending on whether
k=\chi(G)+1
or
k\geq\chi(G)+2
, which we will therefore need to be able to determine.)
We will then find that
G_{P^{\dagger}}
is isomorphic to
G
, while every other candidate graph is isomorphic to either a subgraph of
G
or the complete graph
K_{n}
.
Since
\Delta(G)\neq n-1
, we will conclude that
G
is isomorphic to any largest non-complete candidate graph.

We first show the following, related to the classical theorem of Hajnal and Szemerédi on vertex partitions into independent sets
[
10
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Every graph
G
with with maximum degree
\Delta(G)<\frac{1}{9}n-\frac{1}{3}
has a vertex partition into
\chi(G)
independent sets, each of size at least
4
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta\coloneqq\Delta(G)
.
For each partition
P
of
V(G)
into
\chi(G)
independent sets, let
m\coloneqq m(P)
be the size of a smallest part in
P
, and let
c\coloneqq c(P)
be the number of such parts of minimal size.
Choose a partition
P
of
V(G)
into
\chi(G)
independent sets such that
c
is minimal among the set of such partitions where
m
is maximal.
For each natural number
\ell
, denote by
n_{\ell}
(respectively
n_{\ell}^{+}
) the number of vertices in
G
belonging to a part of size
\ell
(resp. of size at least
\ell
) in
P
.

If
m\geq 4
then we are done.
So suppose
m\leq 3
.
Let
A,B\in P
be distinct, where
A
has size
m
.
Then there is at least one edge between
A
and
B
, since otherwise replacing
A
and
B
with
A\cup B
would yield a partition of
V(G)
into
\chi(G)-1
independent sets.

Suppose
B
has size
m+1
.
Suppose that there is exactly one edge
ab
between
A
and
B
.
Let
C\in P
be distinct from
A
and
B
.
Then
C
contains at least one neighbour of
a
, since otherwise adding
a
to
C
and adding all other vertices of
A
to
B
would give a partition of
V(G)
into
\chi(G)-1
independent sets.
Moreover, if
C
has size
\ell\geq 2m+1
, then
C
must contain at least
\ell-m+1
neighbours of
a
, since if there are
m
vertices
c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}\in C
not adjacent to
a
, then the partition obtained from
P
by replacing
A
,
B
, and
C
with
\{a,c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}\}
,
B\cup A\backslash\{a\}
, and
C\backslash\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}\}
has fewer parts of size
m
than
P
, contradicting the minimality of
P
.
Therefore we can calculate

\displaystyle\Delta\geq d(a)\geq\frac{n_{m}-m}{m}+\sum_{\ell=m+1}^{2m}\frac{n_{\ell}}{\ell}+\sum_{\ell=2m+1}^{\infty}\frac{(\ell-m+1)n_{\ell}}{\ell}\geq\frac{n-m}{2m},

a contradiction since
m\leq 3
.
Therefore there are at least two edges between
A
and
B
in this case.

Suppose
B
has size at least
m+2
.
Suppose there is a vertex
b\in B
with no neighbours in
A
.
Then the partition
Q
obtained from
P
by adding
b
to
A
would satisfy either
c(Q)<c(P)
or
m(Q)>m(P)
, contradicting our choice of
P
in either case.
Therefore, in this case, every
b\in B
is adjacent to at least one vertex in
A
.

Therefore we can calculate

\displaystyle m\Delta\geq\sum_{a\in A}d(a)\geq\frac{n_{m}-m}{m}+\frac{2n_{m+1}}{m+1}+n_{m+2}^{+}\geq\frac{n-m}{m},

a contradiction since
m\leq 3
.
∎

We will use the following terminology and notation throughout the rest of this section.
For each partition
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
, denote by
C_{P}
the number of components of
\mathcal{N}(P)
.
Call a partition
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
a
reconstruction candidate
if
C_{P}=\max_{P^{\prime}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))}C_{P^{\prime}}
, i.e. if the open neighbourhood of
P
has as at least as many components as any other partition of
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
.
For each
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
and
v\in V(G)
, define the
v
-neighbourhood

N_{v}(P)\subseteq N(P)
of
P
to be set of vertices
Q\in N(P)
which can be obtained from
P
by moving
v
.

The following lemma allows us to better understand the nature of reconstruction candidates in
G
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
be a reconstruction candidate.
Then each of the following statements hold.

1.

We have
C_{P}=n
.

2.

Let
A\in P
be a part of size
1
and let
B\in P
be a part of size at most
2
.
Then there is at least one edge
uv\in E(G)
such that
u\in A
and
v\in B
.

3.

Either we have
\lvert P\rvert=k
, or
P
has no parts of size
2
or
3
.

Proof.

The fact that
C_{P}\leq n
follows immediately from Observation
2.2
, which applies for
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
since
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
is an induced subgraph of
\mathcal{B}(G)
.
To show that
C_{P}=n
, it therefore suffices to show that there exists a vertex
P^{\dagger}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
such that
C_{P^{\dagger}}=n
.
By Lemma
3.1
,
G
has an independent set partition
P^{\dagger}
into
\chi(G)
parts, where each part has size at least
4
.
Recall that
\chi(G)<k\leq\chi(G)+1
.
Then, for each
u\in V(G)
, we know that
N_{u}(P^{\dagger})
is non-empty, since the
u
-split neighbour of
P^{\dagger}
belongs to
N_{u}(P^{\dagger})
.
Let
u,v\in V(G)
be distinct and let
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P^{\dagger})
and
Q_{v}\in N_{v}(P^{\dagger})
.
To show that
C_{P}=n
, it suffices to show
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are distinct and not adjacent.
Since every part of
P^{\dagger}
has size at least
4
, there are at least two vertices
u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\dagger}(u)
distinct from both
u
and
v
.
Observe that
Q_{u}(u)\neq Q_{u}(u^{\prime})=Q_{u}(u^{\prime\prime})
, whereas
Q_{v}(u)=Q_{v}(u^{\prime})=Q_{v}(u^{\prime\prime})
.
Therefore, we know that
Q_{u}\neq Q_{v}
, and moreover if
Q_{v}
is adjacent to
Q_{u}
, then
Q_{v}
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by adding
u
to
Q_{u}(u^{\prime})
, which means that
Q_{v}=P^{\dagger}
, a contradiction.

For the third statement, it suffices to show that if either condition fails, then there exist distinct
u,v\in V(G)
such that either
N_{u}(P)\cap N_{v}(P)\neq\emptyset
, or there is an edge from a vertex in
N_{u}(P)
to a vertex in
N_{v}(P)
.
If
P
has two parts
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
such that
uv\notin E(G)
, then the partition
Q_{uv}
obtained by merging
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
satisfies
Q_{uv}\in N_{u}(P)\cap N_{v}(P)
.
If
P
has two parts
\{u\}
and
\{v,w\}
such that
uv,uw\notin E(G)
, then the partition
Q_{vu}
obtained by adding
v
to
\{u\}
and the partition
Q_{wu}
obtained by adding
w
to
\{u\}
are distinct and adjacent.
Suppose
P
has at most
k-1
parts.
For each
u\in V(G)
such that
\lvert P(u)\rvert\geq 2
, let
Q_{u}
denote the
u
-split neighbour of
P
.
If
P
has a part
\{u,v\}
of size
2
, then we have
Q_{u}=Q_{v}
, so therefore
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P)\cap N_{v}(P)
.
If
P
has a part
\{u,v,w\}
of size
3
, then the two partitions
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{v}(P)
are distinct, and are adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
since each can be obtained from the other by moving
w
.
∎

As discussed at the start of this section, our goal now is to examine the neighbourhood at distance
2
of each reconstruction candidate
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
, in order to build a candidate graph
G_{P}
for each such
P
.
In particular, given two vertices
u,v\in V(G)
, in order to determine whether or not
u
and
v
should be adjacent in
G_{P}
, we will consider pairs of neighbours
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{v}(P)
, and look at the set of common neighbours of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
which lie outside of
N[P]
.

Let
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
be given, and let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in N(P)
be neighbours of
P
.
Say that
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are
double-closed
with respect to
P
if they are not adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, and if there exist exactly two vertices of
N(Q_{1})\cap N(Q_{2})
which are not in
N[P]
and which are adjacent to each other. (Observe that there may still be other vertices in
N(Q_{1})\cap N(Q_{2})
.)
For ease of writing, we will not specify that such a pair are double-closed with respect to
P
when the partition
P
is clear from context.

We now begin by studying certain split neighbours of a given partition
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
be a partition of size at most
k-1
.
Let
u,v\in V(G)
be distinct such that
\lvert P(u)\rvert,\lvert P(v)\rvert\geq 4
.
Let
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
be the
u
-split neighbour and
v
-split neighbour of
P
respectively.
Then the following statements hold.

1.

Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are double-closed if and only if
uv\notin E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
.

2.

Every common neighbour of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
belongs to
N[P]
if and only if
uv\in E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert=k-1
.

Proof.

First, observe that
\{u\}\in Q_{u}
and
\lvert Q_{v}(u)\rvert\geq 3
.
Therefore, if
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are adjacent in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, then
Q_{u}
is the
u
-split neighbour of
Q_{v}
, a contradiction since
\{v\}\in Q_{v}
whereas
\{v\}\notin Q_{u}
.
So
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
.

We will now show that there are only four possible common neighbours of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
outside of
N[P]
.
The result will then follow by examining when these common neighbours exist as vertices of
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, and how they relate to each other if so.

Suppose
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
have a common neighbour
R\notin N[P]
.
Observe that we have
\lvert Q_{u}(v)\rvert\geq 3
.
If we have
\lvert R(v)\rvert\geq 3
, then since
\{v\}\in Q_{v}
, we know that
R
is obtained from
Q_{v}
by moving
v
.
But this gives a contradiction, as then we have
R\in N[P]
by Observation
2.2
.
So we have
\lvert R(v)\rvert\leq 2
, which means that
R
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by moving a vertex
x\in Q_{u}(v)
.

Suppose
x=v
.
If
R
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by adding
v
to a part
A\in P
other than
\{u\}
, then we have
\{u\}\in R
and
\lvert Q_{v}(u)\rvert\geq 3
, so
R
is the
u
-split neighbour of
Q_{v}(u)
.
But then
A\cup\{v\}\notin R
, a contradiction.
So
R
is obtained from
Q_{u}
either by splitting
v
, or by adding
v
to
\{u\}
.
Denote the two partitions obtained from
Q_{u}
in this way by
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
respectively, observing that they may or may not exist as vertices in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
.
Indeed, observe that
R_{1}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
if and only if
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
, and observe that
R_{2}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
if and only if
uv\notin E(G)
.
Moreover, observe that if both
R_{1},R_{2}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
, then
R_{1}
is the
u
-split neighbour of
R_{2}
, i.e.
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are adjacent in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
.

Suppose
x\neq v
.
Then, since
\lvert R(v)\rvert\leq 2
, we know that
\lvert P(v)\rvert=4
and
u,v\in P(v)
.
In particular, we have
uv\notin E(G)
in this case.
Let
P(v)=\{u,v,a,b\}
for some vertices
a,b\in V(G)
.
Then we have
x\in\{a,b\}
, and we either have
R(v)=\{v,a\}
, or
R(v)=\{v,b\}
.
Both
\{v\}
and
\{u,a,b\}
are parts of
Q_{v}
, so
R
is obtained from
Q_{v}
by adding the unique vertex of
R(v)\backslash\{v\}
to
\{v\}
.
This means that
R
is uniquely determined by our choice of
x\in\{a,b\}
.
Let
R_{a}
and
R_{b}
respectively denote this partition
R
in the case where
x=a
and
x=b
, observing that
R_{a}
and
R_{b}
may or may not exist as vertices of
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
.
Recall in particular that if
R_{a}
and
R_{b}
are vertices of
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, then
uv\notin E(G)
.
Moreover, observe that if
R
and
R^{\prime}
are distinct vertices from
\{R_{a},R_{b},R_{1},R_{2}\}
such that
\{R,R^{\prime}\}\neq\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
, and if
R,R^{\prime}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
, then
R
and
R^{\prime}
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, since
R
contains a part
\{y,z\}
such that
y
and
z
belong to distinct parts of
R^{\prime}
, each of size
2
.

It follows that
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are double-closed if and only if
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are vertices of
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, which we have shown holds if and only if
uv\notin E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
.
For the second claim, we have shown that if
uv\in E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert=k-1
, then none of
R_{a}
,
R_{b}
,
R_{1}
, or
R_{2}
exist as vertices of
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
, and so every common neighbour of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
belongs to
N[P]
.
On the other hand, we have also shown that if
uv\notin E(G)
, then
R_{2}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))\backslash N[P]
, and if
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
, then
R_{1}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))\backslash N[P]
, so there is a common neighbour of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
outside of
N[P]
in either case.
∎

The following lemma will allow us to use the property of being double-closed in order to detect edges of the underlying graph
G
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
be a reconstruction candidate.
Let
u,v\in V(G)
.
If there exist
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{v}(P)
which are double-closed, then
uv\notin E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
.
Moreover, if every part of
P
has size at least
4
, then if
uv\notin E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
, then there exist
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{v}(P)
which are double-closed.

Proof.

Suppose every part of
P
has size at least
4
, and suppose
uv\notin E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
.
Let
Q_{u}
be the
u
-split neighbour of
P
and let
Q_{v}
be the
v
-split neighbour of
P
.
Then
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are double-closed by Lemma
3.3
.

Now suppose there exist
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{v}(P)
which are double-closed.

Suppose
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are both split neighbours of
P
.
Then
P
has at most
k-1
parts, so since
P
is a reconstruction candidate,
P(u)
and
P(v)
have size at least
4
.
By Lemma
3.3
, we therefore have both
uv\notin E(G)
and
\lvert P\rvert\leq k-2
.

Suppose
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are not both split neighbours of
P
.
Without loss of generality, assume
Q_{u}
is not a split neighbour of
P
.
Then
Q_{u}
is obtained from
P
by adding
u
to some
A\in P
.
Let
R\notin N[P]
be a common neighbour of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
.
By Observation
2.2
we have
R\notin N_{u}(Q_{u})
and
R\notin N_{v}(Q_{v})
since
R\notin N[P]
.

Case 1:
Suppose
Q_{v}
is a split neighbour of
P
.
Then we have
\lvert P\rvert<k
, so we know that
\lvert P(v)\rvert\geq 4
since
P
is a reconstruction candidate.
Since
R\notin N_{v}(Q_{v})
, we know that
\lvert R(v)\rvert\leq 2
.
If
\lvert R(v)\rvert=2
, then
R
is obtained from
Q_{v}
by adding some
v^{\prime}
to
\{v\}
.
In particular, then we have
Q_{u}(u)=Q_{u}(a)
and
R(u)\neq R(a)
for each
a\in A
.
Since
R\notin N_{u}(Q_{u})
, we know
R
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by moving every vertex in
A
.
In particular this means
\lvert A\rvert=1
and
R
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by moving the unique vertex
a\in A
.
Observe that
a\notin P(v)
since
P(v)\geq 4
so
P(v)\neq A
.
Hence
\lvert R(v)\rvert\geq 3
, a contradiction.
So
\lvert R(v)\rvert=1
, meaning
R
is the
v
-split neighbour of
Q_{u}
since
\lvert Q_{u}(v)\rvert\geq 3
.
In particular
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
have at most one common neighbour outside of
N[P]
, contradicting that they are double-closed.

Case 2:

Suppose
Q_{v}
is not a split neighbour of
P
.
Then
Q_{v}
is obtained from
P
by adding
v
to some part
B\in P
.

Our strategy for deriving a contradiction in this case is as follows.
We will show that
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
have a unique common neighbour
R_{1}\notin N[P]
obtained from
Q_{u}
by moving
v
and from
Q_{v}
by moving
u
.
We will then show that any other common neighbour
R_{2}\notin N[P]
of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
cannot be adjacent to
R_{1}
.

Suppose that
R_{1}\notin N[P]
and
R_{1}\in N_{v}(Q_{u})\cap N(Q_{v})
.
If
u\notin B
, define
Q_{u}(B)
to be the part of
Q_{u}
containing
B
(i.e. either
B
or
B\cup\{u\}
).
If
u\in B
, define
Q_{u}(B)\coloneqq B\backslash\{u\}
.
If
Q_{u}(B)=\emptyset
, we claim that
R_{1}
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by splitting
v
.
Otherwise we claim that
R_{1}
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by adding
v
to
Q_{u}(B)
.

Suppose that
B=\{u\}
, i.e. that
Q_{u}(B)=\emptyset
.
Then we have
\{u,v\}\in Q_{v}
, and
\lvert A\rvert\geq 3
by statement
2
of Lemma
3.2
.
This means that
\lvert Q_{u}(u)\rvert=\lvert A\cup\{u\}\rvert\geq 4
, so therefore we have
\lvert R_{1}(u)\rvert\geq 3
, since
R_{1}\in N_{v}(Q_{u})
and
v\neq u
.
Suppose
R_{1}
is not obtained from
Q_{u}
by splitting
v
.
Then
\lvert R_{1}(v)\rvert\geq 2
if
R_{1}(u)\neq R_{1}(v)
and
\lvert R_{1}(v)\rvert\geq 4
if
R_{1}(u)=R_{1}(v)
.
In either case, we have
\{u,v\}\notin Q_{v}
, a contradiction.

Suppose that
B\neq\{u\}
.
If
R_{1}(v)=R_{1}(b)
for all
b\in B
, then
R_{1}
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by adding
v
to
Q_{u}(B)
as claimed.
Suppose there is some
b\in B
such that
R_{1}(v)\neq R_{1}(b)
.
Then
Q_{v}
is obtained from
R_{1}
by adding
b
to
R_{1}(v)
since
B\cup\{v\}\in Q_{v}
and
Q_{v}\notin N_{v}(R_{1})
.
If
R_{1}(v)\neq\{v\}
, then
R_{1}(v)\backslash\{v\}\in Q_{u}
and
R_{1}(v)\backslash\{v\}
is a non-empty subset of
B
, which means
R_{1}(v)\backslash\{v\}=Q_{u}(B)
as claimed.
Suppose
R_{1}(v)=\{v\}
.
Then
B=\{b\}
so
R_{1}(b)\neq\{b\}
by Observation
2.18
.
Since
b\neq u
by hypothesis, and since
R_{1}
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by splitting
v
, this means that
R_{1}(b)=\{u,b\}
, so we have
\{u\}\in Q_{v}
.
This means that either
\{u,v\}\in P
, or
\{u\}\in P
, a contradiction to statement
2
of Lemma
3.2
in either case.

In particular, there is at most one
R_{1}\notin N[P]
such that
R_{1}\in N_{v}(Q_{u})
and
R_{1}\in N(Q_{v})
.
Moreover, any such
R_{1}
satisfies
R_{1}\in N_{u}(Q_{v})
.
Therefore, by symmetry between
u
and
v
, there is at most one
R_{1}\notin N[P]
satisfying both
R_{1}\in N_{v}(Q_{u})
and
R_{1}\in N_{u}(Q_{v})
, and every other
R_{2}\notin N[P]
satisfies
R_{2}\notin N_{u}(Q_{v})
and
R_{2}\notin N_{v}(Q_{u})
.
Observe that such an
R_{1}
exists if and only if
A\neq B
or
uv\notin E(G)
, and let
R_{2}\notin N[P]
be a common neighbour of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
such that
R_{2}\notin N_{u}(Q_{v})
and
R_{2}\notin N_{v}(Q_{u})
.

Suppose
A=B
and
uv\in E(G)
.
Then
P(u)
,
P(v)
, and
A
are all distinct, with
P(v)
,
A\cup\{u\}\in Q_{u}
and
A\cup\{v\}\in Q_{v}
.
Since
R_{2}\notin N_{v}(Q_{u})
and
R_{2}\notin N_{v}(Q_{v})
, moving from
Q_{u}
to
R_{2}
to
Q_{v}
must involve adding every
a\in A
to
Q_{u}(v)
and removing all vertices other than
v
from
Q_{u}(v)
.
Therefore
\lvert A\rvert+\lvert Q_{u}(v)\backslash\{v\}\rvert\leq 2
, which means that
\lvert A\rvert+\lvert P(v)\rvert\leq 3
since
P(v)=Q_{u}(v)
because
P(u)\neq P(v)
.
Hence, by statement
2
of Lemma
3.2
, we have
P(v)=\{v,v^{\prime}\}
and
A=\{a\}
where
av^{\prime}\in E(G)
.
Therefore,
a
cannot be added to
\{v,v^{\prime}\}
.
Observing that
R_{2}\notin N_{v}(Q_{u})
and that the
v^{\prime}
-split neighbour of
Q_{u}
is in
N_{v}(Q_{u})
,
R_{2}
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by adding
v^{\prime}
to some part
C\in Q_{u}
.
Then
Q_{v}
is obtained from
R_{2}
by adding
a
to
\{v\}
.
But we have
a\notin C
since
av^{\prime}\in E(G)
, so we have
C\cup\{v^{\prime}\}\in Q_{v}
, a contradiction as
\{v^{\prime}\}\in Q_{v}
since we know
\{v,v^{\prime}\}\in P
.

Therefore, either
A\neq B
or
uv\notin E(G)
.
So in particular, there is exactly one vertex
R_{1}
such that
R_{1}\in N_{v}(Q_{u})
and
R_{1}\in N_{u}(Q_{v})
.
Since
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are double-closed,
R_{2}
is adjacent to
R_{1}
.
Note that
R_{2}\notin N_{v}(R_{1})
by Observation
2.2
since we have both
Q_{u}\in N_{v}(R_{1})
and
R_{2}\notin N_{v}(Q_{u})
.
The situation is pictured in Figure
5
.

Suppose there is some
b\in B
,
b\neq u
such that
R_{2}(v)\neq R_{2}(b)
.
Then
R_{1}
and
Q_{v}
are both obtained from
R_{2}
by adding
b
to
R_{2}(v)
, meaning
R_{1}=Q_{v}
, a contradiction.
So
R_{2}(v)=R_{2}(b)
for all
b\in B
,
b\neq u
.
But
Q_{u}(v)\neq Q_{u}(b)
for all
b\in B
,
b\neq u
.
Therefore if such a
b
exists, it is unique, and
R_{2}
is obtained from
Q_{u}
by adding
b
to
Q_{u}(v)
.

Suppose
B=\{b\}
for some
b\neq u
.
Then
A\cup\{u,b\}\in R_{2}
and
\{v,b\}\in Q_{v}
.
Since
R_{2}\in N_{b}(Q_{u})
, we have
R_{2}\notin N_{b}(Q_{v})
by Observation
2.2
since
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are not adjacent since they are double-closed.
Therefore we have
A=\{v\}
, a contradiction to statement
2
of Lemma
3.2
.

So
B=\{u\}
or
B=\{b,u\}
for some
b\in V(G)
.
Observe that
\lvert R_{1}(v)\rvert=\lvert B\rvert
in this case.
We have
u,v\in B\cup\{v\}\in Q_{v}
and
A\cup\{u\}\in Q_{u}
.
But
Q_{u}
can be obtained from
Q_{v}
in two steps via
R_{2}
without moving
u
or
v
, so
A=P(v)
.
Therefore
\lvert A\rvert+\lvert B\rvert\geq 4
by statement
2
of Lemma
3.2
.
If
B=\{u\}
, then we have
\lvert Q_{u}(v)\rvert=\lvert A\cup\{u\}\rvert\geq 4
, so we can calculate that
\lvert R_{2}(v)\rvert\geq 3=\lvert R_{1}(v)\rvert+2
.
If
B=\{u,b\}
, then we have
\lvert Q_{u}(v)\rvert\geq 3
, so we can calculate that
\lvert R_{2}(v)\rvert=\lvert Q_{u}(v)\cup\{b\}\rvert\geq 4=\lvert R_{1}(v)\rvert+2
.
However, since we know that
R_{1}\notin N_{v}(R_{2})
, we know that
R_{1}(v)
and
R_{2}(v)
differ in size by at most
1
.
So we have obtained a contradiction in either case.
∎

P
Q_{u}
Q_{v}
R_{1}
R_{2}
u
v
v
u
\overline{v}
\overline{u},\overline{v}
\overline{u},\overline{v}

Figure 5:
A situation in the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
An edge
ST
with a label
x
means
S
and
T
are obtained from each other by moving
x
.
An edge
ST
with a label
\overline{x}
means
S
and
T
are
not
obtained from each other by moving
x
.

Lemma
3.4
will only be useful in the case where we have
k\geq\chi(G)+2
.
We prove the following lemma to deal with the case where
k=\chi(G)+1
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
k=\chi(G)+1
, and let
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
be a reconstruction candidate.
Let
uv
be a non-edge of
G
and let
Q_{u}\in N_{u}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{v}(P)
.
Then
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
have a common neighbour
R\notin N[P]
.

Proof.

Suppose that
P
has a split neighbour
Q
.
Then we have
\lvert P\rvert<\lvert Q\rvert\leq k
, so by statement
3
of Lemma
3.2
, we have
\lvert P(u)\rvert,\lvert P(v)\rvert\geq 4
.

Suppose
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are respectively the
u
-split and
v
-split neighbours of
P
.
Then let
R
be the partition obtained from
Q_{u}
by adding
v
to
\{u\}
.
Otherwise, without loss of generality,
Q_{u}
is obtained from
P
by adding
u
to a part
A\in P
, and
Q_{v}
is either obtained from
P
either by adding
v
to a part
B\in P
, or by splitting
v
. (Observe that
B
need not be distinct from
A
.)
In the former case, let
R
be the partition obtained from
P
by adding
u
to
A
and by adding
v
to
B
.
In the latter case, let
R
be the partition obtained from
P
by adding
u
to
A
and by splitting
v
.

Observe that
R
is an independent set partition of
G
in every case, since
uv\notin E(G)
.
Moreover, observe that if either
Q_{u}
or
Q_{v}
are split neighbours, then
\lvert P\rvert<k
, and so we have
\lvert R\rvert\leq\lvert P\rvert+1\leq k
.
On the other hand, if neither
Q_{u}
nor
Q_{v}
are split neighbours, then we have
\lvert R\rvert\leq\lvert P\rvert\leq k
.
Therefore
R
is a vertex of
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
in every case.
Now suppose that
R\in N[P]
.
If
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are both split neighbours, then
R(u)=\{u,v\}
and
\lvert P(u)\rvert\geq 4
.
So
R
is obtained from
P
by adding
u
to
\{v\}
, which is not possible as
\{v\}\notin P
.
Therefore, without loss of generality,
Q_{u}
is obtained from
P
by adding
u
to some part
A\in P
.
Then, we either have
A\cup\{u\}\in R
, or
A\cup\{u,v\}\in R
, or
A\cup\{u\}\backslash\{v\}\in R
.
Suppose
A=\{a\}
for some
a\in V(G)
.
Then
R
is obtained from
P
by adding
a
either to
\{u\}
or to
\{u,v\}
, a contradiction to statement
2
of Lemma
3.2
in either case.
So
\lvert A\rvert\geq 2
, which means that
R
is obtained from
P
by adding
u
to
A
, which means that
R=Q_{u}
, a contradiction.
So we have
R\notin N[P]
.
∎

We now complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Let
n\coloneq\lvert V(G)\rvert
.
Observe that we can determine
n
from
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
by considering any vertex
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
with a maximal number of components in its neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}(P)
, since then
P
is a reconstruction candidate by definition, so
\mathcal{N}(P)
has precisely
n
components by Lemma
3.2
,

Suppose
k=\chi(G)+1
, and let
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
be a reconstruction candidate.
Let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in N(P)
be neighbours of
P
which are not adjacent to each other.
Then we know that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
each have size at least
\chi(G)\geq\lvert P\rvert-1
, and so are not double-closed by Lemma
3.4
.
On the other hand, if
k>\chi(G)+1
, then for any part
A\in P^{\dagger}
and any two vertices
u,v\in A
, the
u
-split neighbour of
P^{\dagger}
and the
v
-split neighbour of
P^{\dagger}
are double-closed by Lemma
3.3
.
Therefore, we know that
k>\chi(G)+1
if and only if
\mathcal{B}_{k}(G)
contains a partition
P
with two neighbours
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
which are double-closed.
In other words, we can determine whether
k>\chi(G)+1
or
k=\chi(G)+1
.

Now, for each reconstruction candidate
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{k}(G))
, we construct a candidate graph
G_{P}
on vertex set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
as follows.
Let
\zeta
be an arbitrary bijection from
\{1,\ldots,n\}
to the components of
\mathcal{N}(P)
.
If
k=\chi(G)+1
, add an edge
uv
if and only if there exist some
Q_{u}\in N_{\zeta(u)}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{\zeta(v)}(P)
such that every common neighbour of
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
belongs to
N[P]
.
Then, by Lemmas
3.3
and
3.5
,
G_{P}
is isomorphic to a subgraph of
G
, and
G_{P^{\dagger}}
is isomorphic to
G
.
If
k>\chi(G)+1
, add an edge
uv
if and only if for every
Q_{u}\in N_{\zeta(u)}(P)
and
Q_{v}\in N_{\zeta(v)}(P)
, the vertices
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are not double-closed.
Then, by Lemma
3.4
,
G_{P}
is isomorphic either to a subgraph of
G
or to the complete graph
K_{n}
(which happens if
\lvert P\rvert\geq k-1
), and
G_{P^{\dagger}}
is isomorphic to
G
.
Since we know that
G
is not isomorphic to
K_{n}
since
\Delta(G)<\frac{n}{9}-\frac{1}{3}
, we can recognise
G
by selecting any largest non-complete graph among the set of candidate graphs.
∎

Appendix A
Strengthening Theorem
1.5

by giving a complete classification of the pairs of tuples

and

k_{1}
-colouring graph of

k_{2}
-colouring graph of

.

, where

As usual, for each natural number

, denote by

and

the clique and path on

to denote the disjoint union of two graphs

and

.

Theorem A.1
.

For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
G_{i}
be a graph on
n_{i}
vertices and let
k_{i}
be a natural number.
Then
\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{1}}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{2}}}(G_{2})
if and only if at least one of the following conditions holds for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.

1.

k_{i}>n_{i}
.

2.

k_{i}=n_{i}
, or
k_{i}\leq n_{i}
and
G_{i}
is a clique.

3.

}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
.

4.

G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\chi(G_{i})+1\leq k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
, and
n_{1}-k_{1}=n_{2}-k_{2}
.

5.

G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
and
k_{i}\leq\chi(G_{i})
.

6.

k_{i}\leq n_{i}-1
and
{G_{i}^{\prime}}\cong K_{N_{i}-1}+K_{1}
, where
N_{i}\coloneqq\lvert\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{i}}}(G_{i})\rvert
.

7.

k_{i}\leq n_{i}-1
and
{G_{i}^{\prime}}\cong K_{3}+K_{1}
, or
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
and
{G_{i}^{\prime}}\cong\overline{K_{3}}
.

8.

k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
and
{G_{i}^{\prime}}\cong\overline{K_{3}}
, or
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
and
{G_{i}^{\prime}}\cong P_{3}+K_{1}
.

Given any of the eight conditions on
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
k_{1}
, and
k_{2}
in Theorem
A.1
, it will not be difficult to show that
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
are isomorphic.
On the other hand, given an upper-Bell colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
of a graph
G
on
n
vertices, we will show that in all but a small number of cases we can distinguish between the cases where
k\leq n-2
,
k=n-1
, and
k=n
.
(Observe that the case where
k>n
is easy, since
k>n
if and only if
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
contains no vertices.)
The tools developed in Section
2
will then allow us to handle the case where
k\leq n-2
.
Moreover, we will show that in the case where
k=n-1
, we can recognise a partition
P
from which we can determine
G^{\kern 0.40974pt{\hbox to4.37pt{\vbox to3.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.48pt\lower-1.84567pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 1.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 -1.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0.66 0 C 0.66 0.37 0.37 0.66 0 0.66 C -0.37 0.66 -0.66 0.37 -0.66 0 C -0.66 -0.37 -0.37 -0.66 0 -0.66 C 0.37 -0.66 0.66 -0.37 0.66 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

using the local structure of
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
near
P
.
Handling the small number of remaining cases will complete the proof of Theorem
A.1
.

Throughout this appendix, we will use the same terminology introduced at the start of Section
2
.
Given a graph
G
on
n
vertices, we will use
P^{*}_{G}
to represent the partition of
V(G)
into
n
parts, each of size
1
, and omit the subscript when it is clear from context.

.
Therefore, by (
3
), we know that
\mathcal{B}_{\geq n_{1}^{\prime}-1}(G_{1}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq n_{2}^{\prime}-1}(G_{2}^{\prime})
.
The result follows by Observation
2.22
.
∎

We now prove one direction of Theorem
A.1
.

Proof of Theorem
A.1
, ‘if’ direction.

We show that
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
in each of the eight cases. Let
i\in\{1,2\}
be fixed.

1.

Suppose that
k_{i}>n_{i}
.
Then
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{i}}(G)
is the empty graph with no vertices.

2.

Suppose that either
k_{i}=n_{i}
, or
G_{i}
is a clique and
k_{i}\leq n_{i}
.
Then
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{i}}(G)
is a graph on a single vertex.

3.

Suppose that
G^{\kern 0.40974pt{\hbox to4.37pt{\vbox to3.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.48pt\lower-1.84567pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 1.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 -1.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0.66 0 C 0.66 0.37 0.37 0.66 0 0.66 C -0.37 0.66 -0.66 0.37 -0.66 0 C -0.66 -0.37 -0.37 -0.66 0 -0.66 C 0.37 -0.66 0.66 -0.37 0.66 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.72 1.89 M 5.39 1.89 C 5.39 2.26 5.09 2.55 4.72 2.55 C 4.36 2.55 4.06 2.26 4.06 1.89 C 4.06 1.52 4.36 1.23 4.72 1.23 C 5.09 1.23 5.39 1.52 5.39 1.89 Z M 4.72 1.89}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.72 0 M 5.39 0 C 5.39 0.37 5.09 0.66 4.72 0.66 C 4.36 0.66 4.06 0.37 4.06 0 C 4.06 -0.37 4.36 -0.66 4.72 -0.66 C 5.09 -0.66 5.39 -0.37 5.39 0 Z M 4.72 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}_{1}\cong G^{\kern 0.40974pt{\hbox to4.37pt{\vbox to3.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.48pt\lower-1.84567pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 1.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.72 -1.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}_{2}
and
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
.
Then the claim follows by Lemma
A.2
.

4.

Suppose that
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
, and that
\chi(G_{i})+1\leq k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
, and
n_{1}-k_{1}=n_{2}-k_{2}
.
Then the claim follows by Lemma
2.24
.

5.

Suppose that
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
, and that
k_{i}\leq\chi(G_{i})
. Then for each
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{i}}(G_{i})\cong\mathcal{B}(G_{i})
, and so the claim follows by Theorem
1.4
.

6.

Suppose that
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-1
, and that
G_{i}^{\prime}\cong K_{N}+K_{1}
for some
N\geq 1
.
Let
v
be the unique vertex in the
K_{1}
component of
G_{i}^{\prime}
.
Then there are exactly
N
partitions
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
, namely the partition
P^{*}
into
N+1
parts of size
1
, and the
N
partitions where
\lvert P(v)\rvert=2
and every other part has size
1
.
Moreover, each of these partitions are adjacent to each other.
Hence
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{i})
is a clique on
N+1
vertices.

7.

Suppose that either
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-1
and
G_{i}^{\prime}\cong K_{3}+K_{1}
, or
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
and
G_{i}^{\prime}\cong\overline{K_{3}}
.
Then in both cases, there are exactly four partitions of
G_{i}^{\prime}
into at least
k_{i}
parts, and these partitions are all adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{i})
.
Hence
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{i})
is isomorphic to the clique
K_{4}
.

8.

Suppose either
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
and
G_{i}^{\prime}\cong\overline{K_{3}}
, or
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
and
G_{i}^{\prime}\cong P_{3}+K_{1}
.
Then in both cases, there are exactly five partitions of
G_{i}^{\prime}
into at least
k_{i}
parts, and with one exception these partitions are all adjacent to each other in
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{i})
.
Hence
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{i})
is isomorphic to
K_{5}^{-}
, defined as the graph formed by removing any edge from the clique
K_{5}
.∎

It remains to prove the other direction of Theorem
A.1
.
It will be useful to explicitly write down the recovery algorithm we used in the proofs of Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.1
.
Let
G
be a graph on
n
vertices, let
k\leq n-1
be a natural number, and let
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
.
We build a function
\phi_{\mathcal{B}}:V(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathcal{G}
denotes the set of all graphs.
Let
P\in V(\mathcal{B})
be given.
If
\mathcal{N}(P)
is not isomorphic to the line graph of any graph, set
\phi_{\mathcal{B}}(P)
equal to any graph.
On the other hand, suppose
\mathcal{N}(P)
is isomorphic to the line graph
L(H)
of some graph
H
.
Let
H^{\ddagger}
be the graph obtained from
H
by removing all of its isolated vertices, and by replacing all of its triangle components with claw components.
Let
T^{\ddagger}
denote the number of triangles in
H^{\ddagger}
, and let
T_{P}
denote the number of triangles
\Delta
in
\mathcal{N}(P)
such that the three vertices of
\Delta
have a common neighbour outside of
N[P]
.
If
T_{P}-T^{\ddagger}>0
and
H^{\ddagger}
has at least
T_{P}-T^{\ddagger}
claw components, set
\phi_{\mathcal{B}}(P)
to be the complement of the graph obtained from
H^{\ddagger}
by replacing
T_{P}-T^{\ddagger}
claw components with triangle components.
Otherwise, set
\phi_{\mathcal{B}}(P)
equal to the complement of
H^{\ddagger}
.

We will study the behaviour of the functions
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)}
for various upper-Bell colouring graphs
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
.
We first record the following, whose proof follows immediately from the first two paragraphs of the proof of Lemma
2.21
in Subsection
2.3
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
G
be a graph on
n
vertices, and let
k\leq n-2
.
Then the set
\Omega_{5}\subseteq V(\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G))
of
P^{*}
-candidates is non-empty, and we have
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)}(P)\cong G^{\prime}
for every
P\in\Omega_{5}
.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
G
be a graph on
n
vertices.
Then
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{\geq n-1}(G)}(P^{*})\cong G^{\kern 0.40974pt{\hbox to4.37pt{\vbox to3.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.48pt\lower-1.84567pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

}

.

Proof.

Recall that we can write
\overline{G^{\kern 0.40974pt{\hbox to4.37pt{\vbox to3.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.48pt\lower-1.84567pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

, where
\overline{G}^{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\ddagger$}}
is the graph obtained from
\overline{G}
by removing all isolated vertices and replacing all triangle components with claw components.

By Lemma
2.16
, we have
\mathcal{N}(P^{*})\cong L(\overline{G})
.
Since
P^{*}\in V(\mathcal{B}_{\geq n-1}(G))
is a universal vertex, there are no vertices
R\notin N[P^{*}]
.
So we know that
T_{P^{*}}=0
.
Therefore,
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{\geq n-1}(G)}(P^{*})
is the complement of
\overline{G}^{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\ddagger$}}
by definition, as claimed.
∎

As discussed at the start of this appendix, we would like to show that, given an upper Bell
k
-colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
of a graph
G
on
n
vertices, in all but a small number of cases we can determine whether
k\leq n-2
, or
k=n-1
, or
k=n
.
The following lemma shows that the existence of a universal vertex in
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
almost always guarantees that
k-1\leq k\leq n
.
Recall that
K_{5}^{-}
is the graph formed by removing any edge from the clique
K_{5}
.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
G
be a graph on
n
vertices and let
k\leq n
be a natural number.
Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
is not a clique or isomorphic to
K_{5}^{-}
.
Then
k\leq n-2
if and only if
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
contains no universal vertices.

Proof.

If
n-1\leq k\leq n
, then
P^{*}
is a universal vertex.
Now suppose
k\leq n-2
.
We consider two cases.

Case 1:
Suppose there exist two edges
\{u,v\}
and
\{w,x\}
of
\overline{G}
which are not incident with each other.
Let
Q_{1}
be the partition obtained from
P^{*}
by merging
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
.
Let
Q_{2}
be the partition obtained from
P^{*}
by merging
\{w\}
and
\{x\}
.
Let
R
be the partition obtained from
P^{*}
by replacing
\{u\}
,
\{v\}
,
\{w\}
, and
\{x\}
by
\{u,v\}
and
\{w,x\}
.
Observe that
R
is not a neighbour of
P^{*}
, and that
Q_{1}
is not a neighbour of
Q_{2}
.
Moreover,
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are the only common neighbours of
P^{*}
and
R
.
Hence every vertex in
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
has at least one non-neighbour, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
contains no universal vertices.

Case 2:
Suppose all edges of
\overline{G}
are incident with each other.
If
\overline{G}
contains a triangle, then every edge of
\overline{G}
lies in this triangle, so
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
is isomorphic to
K_{5}^{-}
by direct computation, a contradiction.
So
\overline{G}
does not contain a triangle, which means that every edge of
\overline{G}
shares a common vertex
v
.
Therefore, the only vertices of
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
are
P^{*}
and the
\lvert E(\overline{G})\rvert
partitions obtained from
P^{*}
by merging
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
for some
uv\in E(\overline{G})
.
In other words, we can write
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)=N[P^{*}]
. However, by Lemma
2.16
, we know that
N(P^{*})
is a clique, so therefore
N[P^{*}]=\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
is also a clique, a contradiction.
∎

We now turn our attention towards the special case where
k=n-1
for an upper-Bell
k
-colouring graph
\mathcal{B}_{\geq k}(G)
of a graph
G
on
n
vertices.

.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be graphs on
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
vertices respectively and let
k_{1}=n_{1}-1
and
k_{2}=n_{2}-1
.
Suppose

.
Then

.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{1}}(G_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}_{\geq k_{2}}(G_{2})
.
Observe that, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, we know that
P^{*}_{G_{i}}
is a universal vertex in
\mathcal{B}_{i}
since
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
.
By hypothesis, there is a graph isomorphism
\theta:V(\mathcal{B}_{1})\rightarrow V(\mathcal{B}_{2})
.
Therefore,
\theta(P^{*}_{G_{1}})
is a universal vertex in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
.
Hence, the map
\xi:V(\mathcal{B}_{2})\rightarrow V(\mathcal{B}_{2})
which swaps
\theta(P_{G_{1}})
with
P^{*}_{G_{2}}
and sends every other vertex to itself is a graph isomorphism.
It follows that
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}(P^{*}_{G_{2}})\cong\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}(P^{*}_{G_{1}})
, since
\xi\circ\theta
is a graph isomorphism from
V(\mathcal{B}_{1})
to
V(\mathcal{B}_{2})
which carries
P^{*}_{G_{1}}
to
P^{*}_{G_{2}}
.

By Lemma
A.4
, we know that
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}(P_{G_{i}}^{*})\cong G^{\kern 0.40974pt{\hbox to4.37pt{\vbox to3.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.48pt\lower-1.84567pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}_{i}
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.
The result follows.
∎

We now complete the proof of Theorem
A.1
by applying the results we have proved in this section, and tidying up the remaining cases.

Proof of Theorem
A.1
, ‘only if’ direction.

Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{1}}}(G_{1})\cong\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{2}}}(G_{2})
.
Let
i\in\{1,2\}
be fixed.
As shorthand, define
\mathcal{B}_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}_{\geq{k_{i}}}(G_{i})
.

We show that in each of the following cases, at least one of the eight conditions in the statement of Theorem
A.1
holds.

Case 1:
Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{i}
has no vertices.
Then we know that
k_{i}>n_{i}
, so Condition
1
holds.

Case 2:
Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{i}
has exactly one vertex.
Then we know that either
k_{i}=n_{i}
, or
G_{i}
is a clique and
k_{i}\leq n_{i}
, so Condition
2
holds.

Case 3:
Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{i}
has at least two vertices, and is neither a clique nor isomorphic to
K_{5}^{-}
.
Then we know that
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-1
, since otherwise
\mathcal{B}_{i}
would only have one vertex.

•

Subcase 3.2:
Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{i}
contains no universal vertices.
Then by Lemma
A.5
we know that
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
.
By Theorem
1.5
, we know that
G_{1}^{\prime}\cong G_{2}^{\prime}
, and that either
k_{i}\leq\chi(G_{i})
holds, or both
\chi(G_{i})+1\leq k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
and
n_{1}-k_{1}=n_{2}-k_{2}
hold.
In the latter case, Condition
4
holds.
In the former case, Condition
5
holds.

\mathcal{B}_{i}

k_{i}\leq n_{i}-1

\mathcal{B}_{i}
would only have one vertex.

holds.

Case 5:
Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{i}
is isomorphic to
K_{5}^{-}
.
Then we have
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-1
, or else
\mathcal{B}_{i}
would only have one vertex.
Moreover, for each
P\in V(\mathcal{B}_{i})
, by direct computation we know that
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}(P)
is isomorphic either to
\overline{K_{3}}
or
P_{3}+K_{1}
.

If
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
, then by Lemma
A.3
we know that
\Omega_{5}\subseteq V(\mathcal{B}_{i})
is a non-empty set of vertices, and that
\phi_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}(P)\cong G^{\prime}_{i}
for every
P\in\Omega_{5}
.
So therefore
G^{\prime}_{i}
is isomorphic either to
\overline{K_{3}}
, or to
P_{3}+K_{1}
.

, then we know that

\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}_{i}
, we conclude that
G^{\prime}_{i}
is isomorphic either to
\overline{K_{3}}
or to
P_{3}+K_{1}
in this case also.

If
G^{\prime}_{i}
is isomorphic to
\overline{K_{3}}
, then direct computation shows that
\mathcal{B}_{\geq n_{i}-1}(G_{i})\cong K_{4}\ncong K_{5}^{-}
, meaning that
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
in this case.
If
G^{\prime}_{i}
is isomorphic to
P_{3}+K_{1}
, then direct computation shows that if
k_{i}\leq n_{i}-2
, then
\mathcal{B}_{i}
has at least
6
vertices, and so in particular is not isomorphic to
K_{5}^{-}
.
Therefore we know that
k_{i}=n_{i}-1
in this case.
So Condition
8
holds.
∎
